**Which RUN3 §5.2 remedy was chosen for TP-3, and why, stated because the choice was offered and an unstated choice cannot be checked.** RUN3 offered *"a boolean flag, or drop it from the `U` sum"*. **Chosen: the boolean**, which drops it from the sum by construction. **Rejected: plain removal from the line.** AM-20 point 3 puts `unevaluated:list-truncated` in the closed token set *"reported on their own line"*, and removal would leave an architect-ruled token with no report site, which would edge toward re-opening AM-20. The boolean keeps the token, says only what the gate knows (the set was short), and makes `N = E + U` true by construction.

**Which limb gave on TP-4, and why — stated here because ~~the ruling explicitly left the choice to me~~ *(corrected in revision 7, RP4-8: no ruling left me the choice. The panel offered two limbs and the choice is mine by default, as a designer's choice)* and a choice made silently is a choice nobody can check.** The panel offered two remedies: *"Scope the prefix property and its differential test to the **walk** order, or restrict the differential test to equal-bounds runs."*

- **CHOSEN: scope the property to the walk.** Prefix-correctness becomes an **admission** property — *the SET the gate admitted is exactly the first `k` eligible records of the ranked walk over the fetched set* — and stdout is **that set re-sorted into the emission order**. §6.2's differential test is re-aimed to compare the admitted **set** against a walk-order prefix, ~~deriving the walk order from the `gh` stub's own call log (which `test_emission_order_…` already does, so the mechanism is not new)~~ **CORRECTED IN REVISION 7 (RP4-4): not from the call log. D4 admits a trusted-authored record with zero API calls, so the log cannot see it. §6.2 now derives the walk from the fixture and the unbounded run's own output.**
- **REJECTED: restrict the differential test to equal-bounds runs.** Two runs at identical bounds over an identical fixture must produce identical output **for a reason that has nothing to do with prefix-correctness** — it is determinism, and §6.2 already tests it twice (`test_the_gate_keeps_no_resume_cursor`, `test_two_consecutive_runs_both_query_live`). **That option converts the one test AM-33 called the catcher of "a limb nobody thought to enumerate" into a duplicate of two tests that already exist** — and, decisively, **it leaves the broken property statement at `TD:810` standing**, which is the half of TP-4 the panel called *"the underlying property is broken, not just its test."* A remedy that fixes the test and not the sentence it tests is the exact defect this chain keeps finding.

**Facts corrected at every site, not only where a ruling named them.** **(1)** The walk key is **`(rank, number DESC)`** wherever it appears — §2.2.1, §2.3 (D-rank, D5, D5.1 limb (b)), §2.6, §5's interface header, §6.2, §7.2, §9.1. **A coder who finds `updated_at` in a walk-key expression anywhere in this document has found a site revision 6 missed, and that is a defect in this document — with exactly three carve-outs, all of them records of what an earlier document said rather than statements of what the gate does:** the **revision-5 coverage map's AM-29 row** below, the **verbatim quotation of the human's 15:46:50Z directive** in §2.2.1 (which cites AM-29's key because that was the key when they wrote it), and the **verbatim quotation of AM-29's own ruling sentence** in §2.3 Step E. **Each of the three carries a superseded-by-TP-1 marker at the site.** Per the chain's standing rule (AM-38, and revision 5's own handling of revision 4's map), **a historical record is corrected by a correction, never by editing the record into agreement.** **(2)** Every claim resting on `updated_at` being present, non-null or all-distinct is **retired from its load-bearing role while the measurement is kept as recorded fact** — §0.0.4 settlement 1, §0.6 gap 10, §5's boundary note, §6.2 limb (b). **(3)** `docs/v0.7.0/PANEL-1540-S1-S2-dual-lens-adversarial-RUN3.md` joins §9's cited run set.

**One thing revision 6 deliberately does NOT do, stated because the temptation is obvious.** **It does not re-open AM-29's separation of the two orders.** The panel is explicit that TP-4 *"is not a refutation of AM-29"* and that the separation survived direct attack; TP-1's ruling changes **which** key the walk uses, not **whether** the walk has its own key. **Under `(rank, number DESC)` versus `(rank, number ASC)` the two orders are exact reverses within a band, so they disagree on every same-rank pair — they are MORE different than before, not less** — and **Step E remains load-bearing**. A reader who concludes from "both keys are now issue number" that Step E is again a no-op has inverted the finding; §2.3 Step E says so in terms.

**New in revision 6, not in any ruling: one notification and one residual.** **§8/E-11** notifies the `architect` that the human's TP-1 ruling supersedes AM-29 point 1's primary binding — the design is already resolved, no question is asked, and it is recorded rather than left to be discovered in a diff. **§7.2 R-10** records the first residual in this document that is about the **evidence** rather than about the built artefact: #1718 means the adversarial half of all three panel gates was unmeasured, so this chain's finding set is a floor.

**Numbering:** no existing section was renumbered in revision 6. Every amendment and panel cross-reference still resolves. Added: **§0.0.6** (what the human's third-run rulings overtake), **§8/E-11**, **§7.2 R-10**. §9 is **re-scoped for run 4** rather than rewritten; §9.3's register is carried in full, per its own cumulative rule.

The defect is *claimed-changed-but-unchanged* — harmless in effect and corrosive in principle, because the map's whole value is that each row is checkable. The rows are left in place, with this paragraph as their correction, because revision 4's map is a record of what revision 4 said it did.

**A correction to revision 5's own coverage map, made in revision 7 (run 3's TP-8, which revision 6 dropped without a disposition and RUN4 RP4-6(k) found missing).** The **H1 item 3** row above names **§2.7.2** as changed. **Run 3 found §2.7.2 byte-identical between revisions 4 and 5**, so the row is *claimed-changed-but-unchanged*, which is RP-22's exact defect, in the map whose preamble says each row was checked against the diff. **What revision 5 should have changed there, and did not:** §2.7.2's AM-16 reason 2 still says AD-5's digest binding and its ordering invariant *"are precisely what S3 introduces"*. After the human's H1 item 3 (*"no digest-binding fix is being built either"*), that is no longer a scheduled fact. Revision 7 annotates it at the site. The map row is left in place with this paragraph as its correction.

**New in revision 5, not in any ruling: two notifications, no escalated gates.** **§8/E-9** records that I have read AM-31's *"MUST exit 0"* as *"MUST NOT stop the world"* and expressed it as AM-32's explicitly-offered exit `3`, because AM-31 and AM-32 cannot both be satisfied by the integer 0. **§8/E-10** records that the human's *"any candidate authored by an already-trusted identity needs zero further verification"* directive, read as *"author is one of the three HOS App identities"*, would skip verification on **97 of 101** live records and make the gate inert on the entire present queue of the repository it exists to protect — so I have implemented the **AD-2-narrowed** reading (`requester_verdict`, which is the existing D3/D4) and put the discrepancy in front of the human with the numbers attached. **Neither is a gate and neither asks the architect a question.**

Splitting F4 into **F4/F4b/F4c** is required by **AM-34's distinct-token ruling** (one token per condition, and F4 carried three conditions); **F14b** is required by AM-30's budget clamp; and **F37** is the tier form's bot guard, which cannot share F9's row because F9 guards the *file* and F37 guards the *API response*. **None was foreseen when the header was drafted and each is named at its row.**
> 2. **§3's live-row count is 34 and its retired stubs are six.** No prior revision stated either figure, and **a coder writing "one test per row" against a matrix containing six stubs will write six tests for code that must not exist.** §3's preamble carries both counts.

## 0. Verification — what I re-derived, and the four findings that change the design

Every claim below was read from the Worker clone's working tree (revision 1: HEAD `80de8532`). I re-derived only what S1/S2 depend on; I did **not** re-derive the ADR's §0, which is authoritative.

### 0.0.1 What revision 2 re-read (branch `worker-1539-a12-td-revision2-260915124001-1317611`, base `6619e3e2`)

Applying a ruling is not a licence to restate the architect's verified facts as my own. The amendment's §0 verification table is **cited, not re-derived** — FIND-1's and FIND-2's presence in `probe.py`, `ADR-1604` AD-5's and AD-9's text, `ADR-1542` G11's text, and `bin/hos-cron:1137-1138`'s inlined `$(cat …jq)` are all the architect's, verified at `7e5c2a82`. What I read myself this revision, because I am writing new contract text that depends on it:

| Read this revision | What it establishes |
|---|---|
| `scripts/framework/require_human_approval.py:126-138` | The `re.sub(r"\]\s*\[", ",", out)` page-concatenation normaliser AM-5 binds is present, in exactly that form, with its own explanatory comment. `:140-158` `is_bot_reviewer`'s three denylist layers read as the amendment describes (type `Bot`; `[bot]` suffix; `BOT_ACCOUNTS` membership). |

What I read myself this revision, because I am writing **new contract text** that depends on it — two reads, both narrow:

|---|---|---|

> **THE PROVENANCE OF THE HUMAN'S THREE COMMENTS, STATED EXACTLY — because this design turns on the difference between an act performed by a human account and one performed by an App, and it would be indefensible for the document to be loose about it in the one place the distinction is about the ruling itself.**
>
> **All three comments were posted by `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` — the human-proxy App — relaying the human's rulings.** They were **not** typed from `ScottThurlow`'s own GitHub account. **That is the App's sanctioned role** (`CLAUDE.md`, human-proxy session identity: *"carry decisions between the human and the pipeline"*), and this document treats the rulings as the human's because they **are** the human's; what the App did was transcribe them.
>
> **Three things follow, and the second is the one a careless reading gets wrong.**
> 1. **The rulings bind.** A relayed ruling is a ruling. The precedence list's item 1 is correct as written and nothing in this document is softened by the relay.
> 2. **The relay is NOT an authorization act and could not be one, by this design's own rules.** `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` is in `BOT_ACCOUNTS`; `is_bot_reviewer` excludes it unconditionally; **and §2.7.1 states in terms that `edit_issue.sh --app human` "will NOT work" as an approval path for exactly this reason.** **So the same identity that relayed H1's clearance cannot perform the authorizing act that clearance is about** — and **the 49 `scottthurlow-claude[bot]`-authored issues in §0.0.4's distribution are the single largest block the gate exists to hold.** **A reader who conflates "the human ruled, via the App" with "the App may act for the human" has inverted the entire finding.**
> 3. **A clearance is a governance act on a design; an authorization is a mechanical act on an issue.** They travel through different channels, are verified by different means, and **only the second is something this gate reads.** H1 is cleared and **not one issue is thereby authorized.**
>
> **Recorded rather than assumed because I read the comments' author field, not only their text**, and because a document that says *"the human commented"* where the API says `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` is making the same class of claim AF-5 caught: **an act attributed to the person rather than to the account that performed it.**

**The five settlements, stated separately because each one discharges something a prior document could not:**

> **REVISION 6 — SETTLEMENT 1 BELOW IS SUPERSEDED IN ITS CONCLUSION AND PRESERVED IN ITS MEASUREMENT, and the distinction is the whole of how this section must now be read.** The human's TP-1 ruling adopts **AM-29's stated fallback `(rank, number DESC)`** for the walk — **not because `updated_at` was unavailable, but because it is publicly writable.** So settlement 1's *conclusion* (*"the primary binding applies and the fallback is not taken"*) is **withdrawn**: the fallback **is** now taken, on security grounds rather than on availability grounds. Its *measurement* — `updated_at` present, non-null and all-distinct on 101/101 — **stands as recorded fact and is not retracted**; what it loses is its **role**. Nothing in the gate reads `updated_at` after revision 6, so nothing turns on whether the field is present, and **a future reader must not resurrect the primary binding by citing this measurement** — the measurement was never the objection. The panel's own register says it in one line: *"CLOSED — M-1… The fallback is not triggered **on availability grounds** — **though TP-1 now argues for it on security grounds, which is a different reason**."* **The text below is left exactly as revision 5 wrote it, per the chain's supersede-never-overwrite rule (AM-38).**

1. **AM-29's `updated_at` premise — RESOLVED. The primary binding applies; the fallback is NOT taken.** Amendment 4 §0.1 gap 2 records that the field's presence *"is asserted from GitHub's documented issue schema and from this repo's own use of the field on GitHub payloads (`scripts/automation/lib/claim.py:134-141`) — not observed on a Step C response"*, and binds me to *"verify which applies and record the verification"* and to *"not assume."* **Two findings, and they point opposite ways.**
   - **A correction to the architect's cited evidence, made before the measurement rather than after it.** I read `claim.py:125-145`: the field is read off a **comment** payload (`comment.get("updated_at", "")`), with a `.get` default and a `ValueError` guard. I then swept the whole tree: **every in-repo use of `updated_at` on a GitHub payload is on a comment object** — `claim.py`, `test_claim.py:37-38`, `ADR-1540:242`, and the `UNATTENDED-WORKER-*` specs' claim envelope. **Not one is an issue-list payload.** The architect's citation is sound about comments and does not reach the case AM-29 turns on. And `bootstrap/query_issues.sh`'s list mode (`:190-201`) prints a fixed line format that does **not** include the field, so **no sanctioned read path in this repository can observe it**, and `CLAUDE.md` forbids hand-rolling a `gh api` read to get one.
   - **The measurement that settles it.** The dispatching worker ran the Step C query itself — `repos/thurlow-research/HumanOversightSystem/issues?state=open&milestone=2&labels=needs-ai&per_page=100`, paginated, PR records excluded, milestone 2 confirmed as `v0.7.0 — Quality` — and reports **`updated_at` present and non-null on 101 of 101 records**, alongside `user.login` 101/101 and `labels` 101/101. **Provenance: worker, measured 2026-09-16T~15:55Z. Not re-derived by me and labelled as such.** **Therefore AM-29 point 1's primary binding — `(rank, updated_at DESC, number ASC)` — applies, and the `(rank, number DESC)` fallback is not triggered.** A second result worth carrying: **all 101 `updated_at` values are distinct**, so at the present population `number ASC` is a genuine tie-break rather than a load-bearing key — which is exactly the property §2.3 D5.1 limb (b) needs and §6.2 tests.
2. **THE POPULATION HAS CROSSED THE CEILING. RP-2's defect is ARMED, and Amendment 4's present-tense framing of it is out of date.** Amendment 4 §0.1 Correction 1 states that the widening *"displaces nothing today"*, that *"the interaction RP-2 describes has, at this moment, zero effect"*, and that the defect *"arms the moment the population exceeds the ceiling, and the population is two records away."* **The candidate population is 101** (worker, measured 2026-09-16T~15:55Z), against 98 (`pm-agent`/panel, 2026-09-16) and 97 (architect, 2026-09-15). **101 > 100. It is not two records away; it has arrived.** **This strengthens AM-29 rather than weakening it** — the ruling was issued in anticipation of exactly this state and the state now exists — but revision 5 must not, and does not, reproduce the "zero effect today" framing anywhere. **The cost ceiling now binds on a fully-gated walk, today, on this repository.**
3. **The `needs-human` exclusion the human's directive asks for removes ZERO records today.** Of the 101, **0** also carry `needs-human` (worker, measured 2026-09-16T~15:55Z) — unsurprising, because `next_candidates.jq:22` has excluded them since `#901`. **It is a free correctness guard, not a cost saving, and §2.3 D2 already implements it.** It is worth keeping for exactly that reason and it must not be credited with any of the efficiency the human expects (§2.2.1).
4. **The author distribution, which is the measurement that decides how the trusted-author fast path must be read.** Of the 101: **`scottthurlow-claude[bot]` 49, `hos-worker-hos[bot]` 32, `hos-overseer-hos[bot]` 16, `ScottThurlow` 4** (worker, measured 2026-09-16T~15:55Z). **100% of the present backlog is authored by in-house identities and 97 of 101 by bots; zero are externally authored.** This confirms the human's stated premise and, in confirming it, exposes the hazard that **§8/E-10** exists to state: a fast path keyed on *"the author is one of the three HOS App identities"* would skip verification on **97 of 101 records** and make the gate inert on the entire present queue of the repository it was built to protect — and it would exempt the exact block (`scottthurlow-claude[bot]`'s 49) that **AM-1 / H1 item 2 says in terms must be authorized**: *"which, with option C ruled (AM-1), includes the human-proxy App's own filings."* **§2.3 D3/D4 implement the AD-2-narrowed reading and §8/E-10 puts the discrepancy to the human with these numbers attached.** Note the precise present-day effect, because it is smaller than either "0" or "97": under the safe reading the fast path skips **4 of 101** — `ScottThurlow`'s own filings, trusted by **AD-1(a)** at `is_trusted_requester` step 3, which is existing ruled design and not new. The roster is empty and adds none.
5. **`probe.py`'s stderr surface is not needed at all.** AM-18 required the milestone-less skip to emit a diagnostic, and §0.0.2 established that this meant a **new output surface** for a module that writes no stderr and does not import `sys`. **AM-35 retires AM-18, so that requirement goes with it** — verified above that the module's import block is unchanged. This is a real reduction in S1's blast radius and §1.6 records it as one.

**What I could NOT re-derive, stated rather than asserted around.**

1. **The `role_name` field on GitHub's collaborator payload is asserted, not observed.** H3's tier tokens are `read`/`triage`/`write`/`maintain`/`admin`, which are GitHub's `role_name` values rather than the boolean `permissions` object's keys (`pull`/`triage`/`push`/`maintain`/`admin`). **I have not observed a live collaborators response**, no fixture in this repo contains one, and nothing in the tree reads the endpoint. §1.3.2 binds the field and §6.1 carries it as a **named coder acceptance check** of exactly the shape §0.6 gap 5 used to have. **This is revision 5's single most load-bearing unobserved claim and §9 nominates it.**
2. **The installation's actual rate-limit consumption.** AM-30's ≈100-request budget is *"reasoned from a rate limit and a cadence and was not measured against this installation's actual consumption"* — the architect says so themselves and asks the third panel run to attack it (§3(c) item 4). **The overseer's own cron consumption is unaccounted for in it.** I carry it as ruled and nominate it in §9.
3. **Live GitHub repository settings.** Unchanged and declared in every document in this chain. **H3 is now ruled, so ESC-2 no longer turns on them** — but the *collaborator permission graph they define* is now an input to the trust determination, which is a new dependence and §7.2 R-9 records it.
4. **The authorship half of the original P-1 measurement** is superseded by settlement 4 above, which is a direct measurement of the same population through the gate's own query.

**No mutating GitHub write of any kind was performed this session. No issue, comment or label was created. Every GitHub call was a read, through `bootstrap/query_issues.sh --app worker`.**

The 19:55:45Z and 19:56:31Z comments are the newest rulings in the chain and sit at precedence level 0 (§0's binding-inputs list). **They overtake very little, which is itself worth stating: a correction that touched a great deal would not be a correction.** Everything not named below stands unchanged.

The fallback is adopted as the binding walk key**; the primary binding is withdrawn. **AM-29 point 2 (the emission key, and the separation of the two orders) is UNTOUCHED and is re-confirmed by the panel's own rejection list.** The architect is notified at **§8/E-11** |
| **AM-29's promotion-on-authorization property**, and **R-8 mitigation (iii)** which rests on it (*"the human's authorizing act is itself an update, so it lands at the head of its band"*) | **WITHDRAWN, explicitly and with the cost named by the human**: *"Accept losing the promotion-on-authorization property as the cost of closing a zero-privilege, indefinitely-repeatable starvation vector in a security-fix mechanism."* **§7.2 R-8 is rewritten around what is left, and §2.2.1's early-exit check is corrected** |
| **AM-29's closing of RP-2**, as §2.3 Step E states it (*"the act that authorizes a record is the same act that moves it to the head of its walk band"*) | **THE MECHANISM IS WITHDRAWN; the finding does not return in its old form.** RP-2's defect was `number ASC` starving the tail with no recovery act. Under `number DESC` the starved region **moves from the newest records to the oldest**, and the surviving recovery act is a **rank** change rather than an update (§2.3 Step E, §7.2 R-8). **That act requires triage privilege the untrusted author does not have, which is why it is a recovery act and not a second gaming vector** — a design statement of mine, made on top of the ruling and flagged as such. **REVISION 7 (RP4-5, documentary): "recovery act" overclaimed and is struck at §2.2.1, §2.3 Step E and §7.2 R-8.** Within a band there is none. For rank 0 (`priority:critical`) there is none at all, because there is no higher rank. For lower ranks a re-ranked record enters its new band at its own low number, so under `number DESC` the walk reaches it only if fewer than `--max-candidates` eligible records precede it (every higher band plus every newer record in its new band) and the request budget lasts that far. The human's question on whether this cost was part of what the TP-1 ruling accepted is **PENDING** (§7.2 R-8) |

The clamp half is fixed by making a clamp that bites the ceiling stop (run 3's TP-2 remedy). The quarantine half is fixed by restating both halves **over determinations** (§2.3 D5.1). AM-31's behaviour is unchanged. The two halves stay separately named, which is AM-33 point 2's binding content. The architect is notified at §8/E-12 |

RP4-6(l))* | **BOTH ANSWERED** — Q11 **yes**, Q12 **narrow scope, with #1711 carrying the remainder.** The human records that both were answered in conversation and never posted: *"a real gap, my miss."* **Revision 6 posts them into the document; whether they are also posted to #1540 is a worker action, not this document's** |

### 0.2 FIND-1 (severity **HIGH**) — `_verify_codeowner_actor` accepts a `milestoned` event for **any** milestone, including one the CODEOWNER never intended for this work

> **Disposition in revision 5: CLOSED BY DELETION, and AM-4 is RETIRED AS SPENT (AR-7 / AM-35).** Through revision 4 this was closed by **binding** the milestone title (AM-4's inverted default). `pm-agent`'s **AR-7** removed the `milestoned` channel from the authorization disjunction entirely, and **AM-35** rules its mechanics: the arm and its `expected_milestone_title` parameter are **DELETED, not sentinelled** — *"a sentinel leaves a live code path that a single call-site edit re-enables."* **A defect in a deleted branch is not a narrower defect; it is an absent one.** Consequently **AM-4, AM-18, F17, F21, F27, F28, F29 and residual R-7 are RETIRED AS SPENT** — *not overturned*, and the distinction is the whole reason this paragraph exists rather than a deletion. **AM-4 was correct**, and the panel's §2 measurement (97/97 `milestoned` payloads carrying exactly `{"title"}`) showed byte-for-byte title equality was the only binding available. It is retired because its subject no longer exists.
>
> **The analysis below is retained in full and is NOT history-for-its-own-sake.** It is the reason AR-7's narrowing is safe rather than merely cheap: a channel whose *only* discriminator was a title, on a payload nobody in this chain ever observed, laundering a human's benign deferral into an authorization. Delete it and the whole class goes. **A reader who deletes this section will remove the evidence for the ruling and leave only the ruling.**


`probe.py:307-313` collects *every* `milestoned` event on the issue, with no check of which milestone was applied (line range **corrected in revision 5**; revisions 1–4 cited `:283-345`, which is the whole function plus overshoot):

```
elif ev == "milestoned":
    relevant_actors.append(event.get("actor") or {})
```

The events payload carries `event.milestone.title`. The check does not read it. Consequence, on the live path S2 is about to build:

1. A stranger files an issue.
2. The human triages it from their own account to `Backlog` — an entirely benign act, and the *opposite* of authorizing autonomous work on it.
3. A later worker Step 0 cycle re-milestones it to `v0.7.0` and applies `needs-ai` (both bot actions, both correctly non-authorizing on their own).
4. The gate queries `milestone=<v0.7.0>&labels=needs-ai`, finds the issue, walks its events, finds a `milestoned` event by a verified human CODEOWNER, and declares it authorized.

The human's act of *deferring* the request becomes the authorization to build it. This is the same self-authorization shape #1539 exists to close, laundered through one benign human action.

**Revision 5's closure, and it is stronger than revision 4's.** Revision 4 bound the title match. **Revision 5 deletes the branch** (§1.4, §1.6): after AR-7 there is exactly one authorizing signal — *a `labeled` event, for the dispatch label being queried, whose GitHub-reported actor is a verified individual human CODEOWNER* — and the four-step attack above has no step 4. The affected-sign-offs analysis in §7.3 is **restated accordingly** (AM-35, and Amendment 4 §2's affected-sign-offs row 1 binds the restatement): `abef062e`'s `_verify_codeowner_actor` approvals remain **ORPHANED / RE-REVIEW REQUIRED**, but **the re-review's content changes** — it is now against a function whose milestone arm is *absent*, not one whose milestone arm is title-bound, and a re-review written against AM-4's criterion would be checking for a behaviour that no longer exists.

### 0.2.1 New in revision 2 — AF-5, the architect's finding, which invalidated one sentence of my own recommendation

The architect found what I missed: `.github/workflows/label-swap.yml` performs the `/approve` label edit under `GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}` (`:36`, `:74` — I re-read both this revision), so the resulting `labeled` event's actor is `github-actions[bot]`, which `is_bot_reviewer` excludes unconditionally at layers 1 and 2. **A CODEOWNER commenting `/approve` therefore produces no authorization in S2.** My §8/E-1 option C claimed the opposite; that clause is corrected in §2.6 and §8/E-1, and the consequences are specified in §2.7.2. AD-4's own text says "walk the issue's events **and comments**"; revision 1 implemented half of it and did not notice. This is the sharpest single correction in this revision, and I record it as the architect's finding, not mine.

### 0.3 FIND-2 (severity LOW, availability only) — the events fetch is unpaginated

> **Disposition: CONFIRMED by AM-5**, with one addition I did not specify: hitting the page bound MUST emit a stderr diagnostic naming the issue number, because otherwise R-5 is an invisible permanent stall on exactly this repo's most-worked issues. Bound is 10 pages / 1000 events, in the **fetch wrappers only** — AD-1's no-network-in-the-predicate rule holds. Newest-end walking via `Link: rel="last"` is permitted, not required.


Both `_verify_label_actor` and `_verify_codeowner_actor` request `…/events?per_page=100` with no `--paginate`. GitHub returns issue events oldest-first, so on an issue with more than 100 events only the *oldest* 100 are visible and a recent CODEOWNER labelling is invisible. The failure direction is safe (no authorization found → gated) but it is an availability bug on exactly this repo's long-lived, heavily-relabelled issues. §1.4 binds bounded pagination.

### 0.4 FIND-3 (severity **HIGH** for the design, not for security) — AD-2's marker narrowing, read strictly, gates 93% of this repo's open issues

> **Disposition: RULED by AM-1/AM-2/AM-3.** AD-2 stands verbatim; nothing is widened. The subset argument below was adopted as binding reasoning. Option A is rejected as vacuous, option B is **permanently forbidden** by AD-13 as clarified in AM-2, option C is adopted with D as the ergonomic wrapper, and the backfill is bounded by the gate's own query rather than by the open backlog. **Corrected in revision 3 (AF-6):** that boundary rule is right, but the inference AM-3 drew from it — that the bounded set is therefore *small* — is wrong. Measured live by the architect, the bounded set is **≥93 open dispatch-labelled issues in the active milestone**, a page-limited floor. Revision 2's "not 46 issues" framing implied downward and is replaced by the measurement everywhere it appeared. See §2.7. The architect explicitly did **not** route "are human-proxy filings trustworthy?" to the human, because even a maximally favourable answer has no admissible mechanism to express it; what remains the human's is the *consequence*, already on record as ESC-6 item 2. **The 46/100 and four-of-five counts below remain mine and were not re-derived by the architect** — the amendment's §3 flags them as such, and they should be re-derived before the human clears H1, since they size that burden.


The dispatching worker verified live GitHub state this cycle: of 100 open non-PR issues, **46** are authored by `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` (the human-proxy App), **32** by `hos-worker-hos[bot]`, **15** by `hos-overseer-hos[bot]`, **7** by `ScottThurlow`. Of the five current candidate issues, four (#1539, #1340, #1356, #1540) have `scottthurlow-claude[bot]` as both the `needs-ai` and the milestone actor; #1643 has `ScottThurlow` for `needs-ai`; #1678 has `hos-worker-hos[bot]` for both.

AD-1 binds trust to `issue.user`. AD-2 narrows category (b) so a bot-authored issue is trusted only if it *also* matches an enumerated machine-filing marker. The human-proxy App's interactive filings carry no such marker and never will (§1.5 and §8/E-1 explain why no marker can be made to work there). Under a strict reading, four of five live candidates are gated and the fifth survives only on `ScottThurlow`'s own `needs-ai` click.

That is fail-closed and therefore safe, but it is a design outcome the architect must rule on, not one I may resolve by widening the exemption. AD-2 names this exact situation and routes it back to the architect. **§8/E-1 carries it, with my recommendation.** Nothing in §1–§6 widens category (b); this document is written so that E-1's resolution changes *configuration and one prompt/agent behaviour*, not the primitive's shape.

### 0.5 FIND-4 (coordination) — two other accepted designs own the artefact S2 deletes

> **Disposition: RULED by AM-9 (ADR-1604) and AM-11 (ADR-1542 G11).** S2 ships first. `ADR-1604` AD-5's *mechanism* bullet is amended to a single `EXCLUDED_LABELS` entry — no inlined twin, no lock-step divergence test, because there is no second implementation; AD-5's *semantics* are unchanged. G11 is **superseded**, not merely overlapped, and §6.3's conformance test is the required acceptance mechanism. The architect also found a third collision I did not have: `ADR-1604` **AD-9** (§7.3, and D-7 in §1.5.2).


- **`docs/v0.7.0/ADR-1604-worker-self-split-isolation.md` AD-5 and `TECHNICAL-DESIGN-1604-…` §4.6 (Component H)** bind *changes* to `scripts/automation/lib/next_candidates.jq` (new `timeout-stuck` / `blocked` exclusions), to its inlined twin in `worker-cron-prompt.md`, and to `tests/automation/test_next_candidates.py`. S2 deletes all three. Neither has shipped (`next_candidates.jq` still carries only the `needs-human` exclusion).
- **`docs/v0.7.0/ADR-1542-sandbox-script-coverage.md` §4.9 (G11)** plans "one call wrapping the canonical `next_candidates.jq`" as a sandbox-allowlistable wrapper. S2's entry point *is* that wrapper, built for a different reason; G11 is superseded, not merely overlapped.

Both are carried as notifications in §8 (E-3, E-4) with an affected-sign-offs analysis.

**Added in revision 2:**

5. ~~**The shape of GitHub's issue-event `milestone` payload is asserted, not verified.**~~ — **RETIRED in revision 5, with the channel it was about (AR-7 / AM-35), and the retirement is a ruling rather than a deletion.** This gap existed because AM-4 bound exact `milestone.title` equality on a payload nobody in this chain had observed. **The `milestoned` arm is deleted, so the gate never reads a `milestone` object for any authorization purpose and there is no field whose shape matters.** Two things must travel with the retirement rather than be lost with it: **(i) the panel's measurement is PRESERVED, not discarded** — 97/97 `milestoned` payloads carrying exactly `{"title"}` (panel RE-RUN §2) is the only observation of that payload this chain ever made, it settles a question open since this gap was filed, and **S3's `demilestoned` rule will need it** (AM-37, RP-8: *"moot is not the same as wrong, and a measurement thrown away is a measurement re-taken"*); **(ii) the coder acceptance check that hung off this gap is retired with it** — §6.1 no longer asks for a live `milestoned` payload read. **The gate still SELECTS by milestone** (Step C); the milestone loses the power to *authorize*, not the power to *scope* (AM-35's own "What this is NOT").
6. **GitHub's no-duplicate-`labeled`-event behaviour — OPEN, and deliberately non-blocking (updated in revision 3 by AF-7 and AM-14).** The premise: adding a label that is already present produces no new `labeled` event, so the human has no *additive* act available on an issue that already carries the dispatch label. Revision 2 recorded this as asserted from documented idempotency and not observed. **It has since been probed read-only, paginated, across 78 issues: zero duplicate `labeled` events, and zero issues exceeding one page of events (maximum 17)** — the architect's AF-7, cited not re-run. That is the strongest read-only evidence this corpus can produce, and it **raises confidence without closing the gap**, for a reason no further scanning of any width addresses: **absence of a duplicate event may reflect absence of the *attempt* rather than suppression of the event.** Nobody in this repo's history has had a reason to re-apply an already-present label, so a corpus with no attempts and a corpus with suppressed events are indistinguishable.
   - **Nothing in this design waits on it.** AM-15's instruction wording (§2.7.4) is constructed to be correct whichever way it resolves, because it routes on what the GitHub UI actually offers rather than on the idempotency fact. That is what makes an open gap non-blocking rather than a deferred defect.
   - **The decisive check is a live *mutating* re-apply, and its scope is binding (AM-14):** apply a label an issue already carries, then read that issue's event list — paginated, from the newest end — for a second `labeled` event with no intervening `unlabeled`. It is run by the **`coder`, under a bot identity, as a named S2 acceptance check** (§6.2), **on a throwaway surface only** — a scratch issue, or a scratch label on an already-closed issue — and **never on a live candidate issue**, because after S2 a stray `unlabeled`/`labeled` pair by a human account on a real issue is indistinguishable from a cutover authorization act. The idempotency behaviour under test is a property of the GitHub API, not of the actor, so a bot-actor probe answers it at strictly lower cost and with no human time.
   - **It is a confirmation, not a precondition.** If a bare re-apply *does* emit a fresh event, E-5's two-act cost halves and the second act becomes unnecessary — AM-14's lazy-drain default is unaffected, because it was never justified by the two-act cost alone. The result is reported on #1539 either way; a gap that has been closed should stop being carried as open.
7. I did not re-derive either amendment's verification table (§0.0.1 and §0.0.2 say what I did read, and why). The architect's §0 in each stands as their verification, cited.

**Added in revision 5 — two new gaps, both created by rulings made after revision 4, and one closed:**

8. **NEW, and it is the load-bearing one: GitHub's collaborator-permission payload shape is asserted, not observed.** The human's H3 addendum fixes the roster's tier tokens to GitHub's own permission-level names — `read`, `triage`, `write`, `maintain`, `admin` — *"no translation layer, no ambiguity."* Those five strings are GitHub's **`role_name`** values; the `permissions` object on the same payload is a different vocabulary (`pull`/`triage`/`push`/`maintain`/`admin`), and a coder who matches against **that** would silently fail every `read` and `write` entry in a consumer's roster while passing every test written against a fixture the same coder wrote. **I have not observed a live collaborators response** and no fixture in this repo contains one. ~~and nothing in the tree calls the endpoint~~ — **CORRECTED IN REVISION 6 (TP-5): that clause is wrong as written. Nothing calls the LIST endpoint this design uses; `scripts/framework/provision_agent_account.sh:190` calls the PER-USER sibling and reads the LEGACY `.permission` vocabulary, so its own `maintain` arms can never fire** (§1.3.2, confirmed at the line by me this session). **The unobserved claim survives the correction intact — `role_name` is still unobserved on a live payload from this installation — and the correction makes the gap sharper rather than smaller, because the repo's one live caller of the adjacent endpoint reads the wrong vocabulary today.** **§1.3.2 binds `role_name` and §6.1 carries this as a NAMED CODER ACCEPTANCE CHECK of exactly the shape gap 5 used to have**: observe one live response, confirm `role_name` carries the five documented values, and **if it does not, raise the discrepancy to `technical-design` rather than substituting the `permissions` object on your own judgement.** The failure direction if the check is skipped and the field is wrong is **fail-closed** — an unmatched tier contributes nobody — which is why this is a declared gap rather than a blocker, and §9 nominates it as revision 5's least-grounded claim.
9. **NEW: the ≈100-request budget share is reasoned, not measured against this installation's actual consumption.** AM-30 derives it from a 5,000/hour installation limit and a 12-cycle/hour cadence. ~~**The overseer's own cron consumption is unaccounted for**, the architect says so themselves, and Amendment 4 §3(c) item 4 asks the third panel run to attack precisely this.~~ **CORRECTED IN REVISION 7 (RP4-6(h)): run 3's M-5 settled the overseer half.** The overseer is a separate GitHub App with its own 5,000/hour installation bucket (`bootstrap/get_app_token.sh` resolves a distinct App per role), so it never drew on the worker's budget. **What survives is the budget SHARE.** The ≈24% is still a choice rather than a measurement. It is now a choice about a bucket whose other consumer is the rest of the worker App's own cycle, measured live by run 3 at 5,000/hour (M-3), at a cadence run 3 measured at half the assumed rate (M-4, every 10 minutes). **I carry the bound as ruled and do not launder the derivation as a measurement.** Note that the rate-limit figure itself has no citation at the site Amendment 4 names: it attributes the figure to `docs/OVERSIGHT-RUNBOOK.md:56`, and **`:56` is a crontab example line — the runbook states the five-minute cadence, not the rate limit.** ~~I checked; the only in-repo statement of a 5,000/hour core-API bucket is `docs/specs/UNATTENDED-WORKER-PROTOCOL.md:448`.~~ **CORRECTED IN REVISION 7 (RP4-6(k), run 3's TP-10(ii)): "the only" was false.** The bucket is stated at `docs/specs/UNATTENDED-WORKER-PROTOCOL.md:448` **and** at `docs/specs/UNATTENDED-WORKER-TECH-DESIGN.md:256`. The second site also defines a per-customer request budget at `:257`, prior art AM-30 did not cite. Both were re-read at those lines this session. The cadence half of the derivation is cited correctly; the limit half is GitHub's documented figure, cited to the wrong file.
10. **CLOSED in revision 5: the `updated_at` presence question** that AM-29 raised and bound me to settle. See §0.0.4 settlement 1 — present and non-null on 101/101, all values distinct. ~~**The primary binding applies and the fallback is not taken.**~~ **MOOT IN REVISION 6, and moot is not the same as wrong.** The human's TP-1 ruling adopts the `(rank, number DESC)` fallback on **security** grounds — the field is advanced by any GitHub account posting a comment — so **the gate no longer reads `updated_at` at all and this gap governs nothing.** The measurement stands as recorded fact and travels with the record (AM-37/RP-8: *"a measurement thrown away is a measurement re-taken"*); **its role does not.** Note the shape, because it is the third time this chain has produced it: **a gap was closed by measuring the thing it named, and the thing it named turned out not to be the objection.** Nobody had asked *who can write the key*, which is the question the panel asked from four independent chunks.

## 1. S1 — the trust primitive

### 1.1 File layout (binding — AF-3; placement is load-bearing, do not relocate for import convenience)

| Path | Status | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `scripts/framework/requester_trust.py` | **new** | The single implementation of the requester-trust question: loaders, the pure predicate, the AD-2 marker table, the **permission-tier resolver** (H3, §1.3.2), and the pure CODEOWNER-actor verifier. |
| `scripts/framework/trusted-requesters.txt` | **new, empty of entries** | AD-1(c) roster. Header comment only; zero entries. **Its entry format changes in revision 5 (H3): an entry is an explicit login OR a GitHub permission-tier token.** |
| `scripts/framework/trusted-requesters.txt.example` | **new — GIVEN A HOME IN REVISION 5 (RP-19)** | The consumer-facing format template. **Revision 4 ruled it into the ship list (§2.1, §4.3) and then listed it in no file-layout table and no slice** — the panel is right that `test_every_listed_file_exists_in_source` fails the moment the ship list names a file nothing creates. **It is created in S1**, beside the live roster whose format it documents, and it carries both entry forms plus the same header the live file carries. Its content obligation grows under H3: a consumer cannot be expected to guess that `write` is a tier token and `Write` is a login. |
| `scripts/automation/lib/probe.py` | **modified** | `_codeowners_humans` becomes a re-export of the shared function; `_verify_codeowner_actor` becomes a thin fetch-wrapper delegating its decision to the shared pure function, **with its `milestoned` arm and `expected_milestone_title` parameter DELETED (AR-7 / AM-35)** and its docstring corrected. No decision logic remains in this file. **It gains no stderr surface** — see §1.6. |

**Import direction, binding.** `requester_trust.py` sits on a protected surface and therefore MUST NOT import from `scripts/automation/**` or `scripts/oversight/**` — neither is protected, so importing either would let a bot weaken the gate's dependencies without a human approval. Permitted imports: the standard library, and `scripts.framework.require_human_approval.is_bot_reviewer`. The dependency arrow is `probe.py → requester_trust.py`, never the reverse; `probe.py` already imports `require_human_approval`, so this is the established direction, not a new one.

There is no `scripts/framework/__init__.py`; `scripts/__init__.py` exists and `scripts.framework` resolves as a namespace package. `from scripts.framework.requester_trust import …` and `python3 -m scripts.framework.…` both work from the repo root today (verified this session against `scripts.framework.require_human_approval`).

### 1.2 Data types

**Changed in revision 5 by the human's H3 ruling.** `TrustedSet.roster` is no longer a flat set of logins: the committed roster now records a **policy**, part of which is a set of GitHub permission tiers whose *membership* is resolved live. The type carries the resolved membership as its own field so that **the pure predicate never learns where a login came from** and never performs I/O to find out.

```
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TrustedSet:
    codeowners: frozenset[str]   # AD-1(a) — lowercased logins, '@' stripped
    roster:     frozenset[str]   # AD-1(c) — EXPLICIT logins from the roster file
    tier_members: frozenset[str] # AD-1(c) via H3 — logins RESOLVED from the
                                 #   roster's tier tokens against the live
                                 #   collaborator-permission API (§1.3.2).
                                 #   EMPTY when no tier token is listed, and
                                 #   EMPTY when resolution failed. NEVER a
                                 #   snapshot read from a file.
    tier_of:    Mapping[str, str]  # NEW IN REVISION 7 (RP4-6(a)): login -> the
                                 #   ONE tier token that admitted it (role_name
                                 #   matching is exact, so a login matches at
                                 #   most one tier). INVARIANT, asserted at
                                 #   construction: frozenset(tier_of) ==
                                 #   tier_members. Read ONLY to build the
                                 #   `roster-tier:<tier>` reason token; never
                                 #   consulted for membership. Store it
                                 #   read-only (e.g. MappingProxyType); do not
                                 #   hash TrustedSet.
    tiers:      frozenset[str]   # the tier TOKENS the roster listed, verbatim —
                                 #   carried for reporting and for the reason
                                 #   token only, NEVER consulted for membership
    apps:       frozenset[str]   # AD-1(b) — lowercased logins, COPILOT excluded
    bots:       frozenset[str]   # BOT_ACCOUNTS, lowercased — EXCLUSION input only

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MachineFilingMarker:
    marker_id:      str          # stable audit id, e.g. "hos-cron/baseline-red"
    title_prefix:   str          # anchored match against issue.title
    title_contains: str | None   # optional second required substring
    emitting_site:  str          # "path::symbol" — drives the conformance test
    status:         str          # "active" | "dormant"

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RequesterVerdict:
    trusted:   bool
    reason:    str               # stable machine-readable token, never prose
    category:  str | None        # "codeowner" | "roster" | "trusted-app" | None
    marker_id: str | None
```

`TrustedSet.bots` lives inside `TrustedSet` for one reason only: so that a caller cannot construct a trusted set without also supplying the exclusion input. It is **never** consulted as a trust source. Any code path that reads `.bots` to decide eligibility is a defect.

**Three boundaries `tier_members` must honour, and they are the reason it is a separate field rather than merged into `roster` at load time (H3, binding).**

1. **It is resolved, never stored.** `tier_members` is populated on every construction of a `TrustedSet` from a live read (§1.3.2). **There is no file, no cache, no TTL and no snapshot**, which is FR7's rule applied one layer out: a snapshot of tier membership would be a cached trust determination wearing a configuration costume, and it would let a revoked collaborator stay trusted until someone remembered to re-run a script.
2. **It is EMPTY on any failure.** An unreachable, non-JSON, rate-limited or otherwise failed resolution yields the empty set — never a partial one, never a stale one, never an exception that reaches the predicate. The direction is the only one available: an empty `tier_members` can only ever **reduce** who is trusted. §1.3.2 states what the gate reports when this happens, because *fail-closed and silent* is the combination this chain keeps finding.
3. **It never confers authorization.** The human attached this note to the ruling themselves and it is repeated here at the type: *"this ruling is scoped entirely to the **requester-trust** roster's own entry format, not to who may authorize."* `tier_members` is an input to `is_trusted_requester` (AD-1(c)) and **to nothing else**. It is not consulted by `verify_codeowner_actor`, it is not a CODEOWNERS substitute, and a record authored by a tier member is trusted **as a requester** exactly as a roster-listed human is — which under **AR-7** is a statement about whether the record needs an authorizing act, not a statement that one occurred. §1.4 makes this structural; §6.1 pins it with a test that `tier_members` appears in no argument list of `verify_codeowner_actor`.

**`tiers` versus `tier_members`, because conflating them is how this gets implemented wrong.** `tiers` holds the tokens the file listed (`{"write", "admin"}`); `tier_members` holds the logins the API resolved. **Only `tier_members` decides anything.** `tiers` exists so the gate can report *which policy* admitted a requester (`roster-tier:write`) and so an operator reading the summary can tell an empty roster from a roster whose tier resolution failed. **Any code path that tests membership against `.tiers` is a defect** — it would be comparing a login against a permission name.

**Reason tokens (closed set, binding). One is added in revision 5 (H3).** `"codeowner"`, `"roster"`, **`"roster-tier:<tier>"`**, `"trusted-app"`, `"trusted-app:<marker_id>"`, `"not-in-trusted-set"`, `"no-login"`, `"bot-in-human-category"`, `"trusted-app-no-machine-filing-marker"`. Downstream (S6 audit) parses these tokens; they are an interface, not a log message, and must not be reworded without updating consumers. **`roster-tier:<tier>` carries the tier that admitted the login** — `roster-tier:write` — because *"this requester is trusted"* and *"this requester is trusted because your policy trusts everyone with push access"* are different facts, and only the second is reviewable after the fact. *(Revision 5 note: the S5 assessment header is no longer a consumer of these tokens — the human's H1 item 3 defers the assessor indefinitely. S6 remains.)*

**FOUR UNEVALUATED-reason tokens, one added in revision 5 (AM-31). The distinction between them and the nine above is the whole of AM-20's ruling:**

```
unevaluated:sufficient       # the walk stopped because enough eligible candidates were found
unevaluated:cost-ceiling     # the walk stopped because the API-request ceiling was reached
                             #   REVISION 7 (TP-2 / RP4-1(i)): this INCLUDES the at most
                             #   ONE record whose events fetch the budget clamp cut short
                             #   before a match (§1.4 rule 4). That record is named on its
                             #   own line, `unevaluated:cost-ceiling issue=#<n>`, and is
                             #   NEVER gated. The clamp biting IS the ceiling stop
unevaluated:list-truncated   # the record was never on the candidate page set at all (AM-22)
                             #   REVISION 7 (TP-3): a SET-LEVEL BOOLEAN FLAG, NOT a
                             #   count. Reported as `unevaluated:list-truncated=<yes|no>`.
                             #   It is NOT summed into U. The records it describes are
                             #   outside N, and their number cannot be derived (AM-31)
unevaluated:query-failed     # NEW (AM-31) — this record's events query failed, so the gate
                             #   QUARANTINED it and CONTINUED the walk. It is counted here,
                             #   NEVER as `gated`, and it is reported WITH ITS ISSUE NUMBER
```

**They are unevaluated-reasons, not gate-reasons, and the difference is load-bearing rather than taxonomic.** The eight tokens above are **determinations**: the gate looked at the record and concluded it is held. The three above are **absences of a determination**: the gate never looked. The panel found that revision 3's closed set had no token for a record the cap skipped and that the design did not say whether such records counted toward `gated=<G>` — and **both available answers were wrong**. Counted, `gated` overstates the human's queue and mislabels a *starved* issue as *"held for human authorization"*, which is a false statement about a person's obligation. Uncounted, the one load-bearing signal of the S2 era silently undercounts. AM-13 made that line load-bearing; a load-bearing line that can be wrong in two directions is not a signal.

**Three binding consequences for every consumer of these tokens:**

1. **An unevaluated token MUST NOT appear in the `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED reasons:` breakdown** (§2.3 Step F). That line enumerates determinations only. Unevaluated records are reported on their own line, with their own count.
2. **`gated=<G>` counts determinations only.** A record the walk never reached is **not** gated; it is **unevaluated**, and it is counted in `unevaluated=<U>`.
3. **`no-codeowner-actor` remains what it is today** — a gate-local D5.3 determination token (**renumbered from D5.5 in revision 5** — see §2.3 D5, where RP-17's duplicated item and its off-by-one citations are fixed), not a `RequesterVerdict.reason` and not an unevaluated-reason. It means the gate ran the authorization check and found no qualifying actor. That is a determination, and it belongs in the `gated` count.
4. **`unevaluated:query-failed` is an ABSENCE of a determination and it is a per-record token, which makes it unlike the other three (AM-31, binding).** `sufficient`, `cost-ceiling` and `list-truncated` describe *why the walk stopped or was short*; `query-failed` describes *one named record the walk skipped over while continuing*. **It MUST be reported with the issue number** — `unevaluated:query-failed issue=#<n>` — because the operator's remedy is per-record, and a count alone tells them a number without telling them which one. It is the one unevaluated reason that is an **anomaly** rather than a routine (AM-30 point 3), and it keeps the loudest report class.
5. **Their report classes are NOT uniform, and revision 5 splits them (AM-30 point 3, binding).** `unevaluated:sufficient` and `unevaluated:cost-ceiling` are **routine** — they appear on the summary line and the `UNEVALUATED` line and **MUST NOT emit a `WARN`**. `unevaluated:list-truncated` and `unevaluated:query-failed` are **anomalies** and do. The reason is AM-24's own argument turned on its author: *"a warning that fires on every cycle of correct operation is not a warning; it is a banner, and by the second week it is invisible."* Revision 4 gave the cost ceiling a `WARN` on the premise that reaching it was exceptional; **AM-30 withdraws that premise** (*"the ceiling WILL bind in normal operation"*), and **§0.0.4's measurement shows the population has already crossed it** — so the `WARN` would have started firing on the first fully-gated cycle after merge and never stopped.
6. **NEW IN REVISION 7 (run 3's TP-3, reinstated by RUN4 RP4-3): `unevaluated:list-truncated` is a SET-LEVEL BOOLEAN, reported as `unevaluated:list-truncated=<yes|no>`, and it is NOT part of the `U` sum.** Through revision 6 it was printed as a count `<y>` inside `UNEVALUATED <U> of <N>`. That was wrong twice over. The records it describes were never returned by Step C, so they are not in `N`, and summing them into `U` breaks `N = E + U`. And their number cannot be derived, because GitHub's `/issues` returns no total, and AM-31 forbids stating a count the gate cannot derive. **The partition is now exact: `U = sufficient + cost-ceiling + query-failed`, all three counted over records Step C returned.** The flag carries the one fact the gate does know: that the set was short.
   **Why a boolean rather than dropping it from the line (RUN3 §5.2 offered both).** AM-20 point 3 makes `unevaluated:list-truncated` one of the closed-set unevaluated-reason tokens *"reported on their own line"*. Dropping it from the `UNEVALUATED` line would leave an architect-ruled token with no report site, and that edges toward re-opening AM-20. A boolean keeps the token and its line. It states nothing AM-31 forbids, and it leaves `N = E + U` true by construction. `WARN candidate-list-truncated` (F33) remains the loud, always-emitted signal. The flag is its echo on the line an operator reads for "why is this answer partial".
7. **NEW IN REVISION 7 (run 3's TP-2, reinstated by RUN4 RP4-3, and the clamp half of RP4-1): a record whose events fetch the budget clamp cut short before a match is `unevaluated:cost-ceiling`, NEVER `gated`, and the clamp biting IS the ceiling stop.** Through revision 6 §1.4 rule 4 made that record *"identical to rule 1 — gated"*. That recorded a determination the gate did not make: the authorizing event may sit on the page the clamp withheld. It also made the verdict depend on walk position rather than on repository state, and it counted as *"held for human authorization"* a record a complete walk would authorize. That is the false statement about a person's queue that consequence 2 above forbids. **The clamped record is the one exception to "cost-ceiling is only a count":** it is named on its own line, `unevaluated:cost-ceiling issue=#<n>`, in the **routine** class (no `WARN` prefix, per consequence 5). **There is at most one per run**, because the walk ends there. `query-failed` remains the only per-record reason that can occur more than once.

*Traceability (AR-5):* these tokens are the vocabulary **FR27** is satisfied through in the S2 era (AR-6 marks FR27 **Partly** — Step F's summary and `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` lines are the S2 half; AD-11's full held-request listing is S6).

### 1.3 Loaders

All loaders take `repo_root: str = "."` and are pure with respect to the environment except where stated. All are fail-closed by construction: an absent source yields an **empty** category, and an empty category can only ever *reduce* who is trusted.

**`codeowners_humans(repo_root=".") -> set[str]`** — promoted verbatim from `probe.py:_codeowners_humans`. Read `<repo_root>/.github/CODEOWNERS`; skip blank lines and `#` comments; for each remaining line treat fields *after the first* (the path) as owners; strip a leading `@`; **skip any token containing `/`** (an `org/team` pattern is not an individual human — AF-2, and the reason no glob matcher is needed anywhere in this design); lowercase; return the set. `not path.is_file()` → empty set. Any other read error propagates to the caller. Behaviour is byte-identical to the shipped function so that `tests/automation/test_probe.py::TestCodeownersActorVerification`'s three existing cases pass unmodified against the new home.

**`load_trusted_apps(repo_root=".") -> set[str]`** — read `<repo_root>/scripts/framework/machine-accounts.env` and extract **exactly three** variables: `BOT_WORKER_USERNAME`, `BOT_OVERSEER_USERNAME`, `BOT_HUMAN_USERNAME`. Lowercase, strip surrounding quotes and trailing `#` comments. Binding constraints:

- `COPILOT_BOT_LOGIN` is **not** read (FR2(b): a third-party reviewer is not an HOS role). Neither is the composite `BOT_ACCOUNTS`.
- The file MUST be **parsed with a line regex, never `source`d.** Shell-executing a config file on an authorization path turns a config edit into code execution. `label-swap.yml` sources it today; that is a workflow running from the trusted default branch, a different threat model, and is S3's business (AD-6), not ours.
- Missing file → empty set.
- **No environment override.** AD-13 forbids any knob that widens the trusted set; an env var naming a fourth trusted app is exactly that.

**`load_bot_accounts(repo_root=".") -> set[str]`** — the exclusion input for `is_bot_reviewer`. Baseline = the four logins in `machine-accounts.env` (`BOT_WORKER_USERNAME`, `BOT_OVERSEER_USERNAME`, `BOT_HUMAN_USERNAME`, `COPILOT_BOT_LOGIN`). If the `BOT_ACCOUNTS` environment variable is non-empty, the result is the **union** of baseline and env — never the env alone.

Rationale, binding (AD-13): a *larger* denylist is strictly stricter, so the environment may add. Allowing the environment to *replace* would let an operator (or a compromised cron environment) shrink the denylist and thereby promote a bot to "human" — a widening knob wearing a narrowing costume. If the file baseline is empty, callers fail closed regardless of the environment (§2.3, step B).

**`load_trusted_requesters(repo_root=".") -> RosterPolicy`** — **SIGNATURE CHANGED IN REVISION 5 (the human's H3 ruling).** Read `<repo_root>/scripts/framework/trusted-requesters.txt` and return **both** halves of the policy it records:

```
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RosterPolicy:
    logins: frozenset[str]   # explicit named users, lowercased
    tiers:  frozenset[str]   # tier tokens, verbatim from PERMISSION_TIERS
```

**Format, binding. Two entry forms, and FR3's per-entry provenance is required of BOTH** — the tier form needs it *more*, not less, because a tier entry is a standing delegation to whoever holds that permission tomorrow rather than a decision about one named person:

```
<login>          # added-by: <login> added: <YYYY-MM-DD> why: <free text>
tier:<name>      # added-by: <login> added: <YYYY-MM-DD> why: <free text>
```

- **`PERMISSION_TIERS = ("read", "triage", "write", "maintain", "admin")`** — a module constant, and **the closed set of legal tier names**. These are **GitHub's own permission-level names, used directly**, per the human's 07:16:58Z addendum: *"Don't invent separate names ('Collaborators'/'Maintainers'/'Admins') that then need a mapping table … No translation layer, no ambiguity."* **There is no mapping table in this design and there must never be one.**
- **The `tier:` prefix is required and is the discriminator.** A bare `write` on its own line is parsed as **a login named `write`**, not as a tier — deliberately, and this is the one place the human's "no translation layer" instruction needs a design decision rather than a transcription. GitHub logins and permission names occupy the same string space (`admin` is a valid GitHub login), so a format that distinguished them by *value* would make the roster's meaning depend on GitHub's account namespace. **The prefix makes the distinction lexical and local.** It is one token of ceremony against a class of error that would be invisible in review and silent at runtime.
- **An unrecognised tier name is a MALFORMED LINE, not an unknown tier**: `tier:owner`, `tier:push`, `tier:collaborator` are all **skipped with a diagnostic naming the line number and listing the five legal values.** Never silently ignored, and never passed through to the API to see what happens. *(Case: tier tokens are matched **case-insensitively** after the prefix and normalised to lowercase, so **`tier:Write` is ACCEPTED as `write`** and is not malformed. Logins are lowercased as they already are.)* *(Corrected in revision 7, RP4-6(b): revision 5 listed `tier:Write` among the malformed examples, then said in the same parenthetical that it is accepted. It is accepted, as F34 and §6.1's `test_tier_token_matching_is_case_insensitive` already say. It is replaced in the example list by `tier:collaborator`, the example §6.1's F34 test already uses.)*
- Blank lines and whole-line `#` comments are ignored.
- A line matching neither form is **skipped, with a one-line diagnostic to stderr naming the line number**. It is never accepted on a best-effort read. **A malformed roster grants nothing.**
- An entry whose login ends in `[bot]`, or for which `is_bot_reviewer(login, "", bots)` is true, is **rejected with a diagnostic**. Without this the roster is a trivial route around AD-2: add the worker bot and every worker-authored issue becomes trusted with no marker. **The roster is for people.** *(This guard applies to the explicit-login form. The tier form is guarded at resolution time instead — §1.3.2 — because the bot filter must be applied to what the API returns, not to what the file says.)*
- Missing file → **`RosterPolicy(frozenset(), frozenset())`** (FR3: the mechanism must still function with CODEOWNERS + apps as the trusted set).
- A read error that is not `FileNotFoundError` propagates.

**The shipped file contains a header comment and zero entries.** Its header states, in the file: that it is a protected surface; that entries require human approval; that bot logins are rejected by the parser; that membership confers **requester** trust only, never approver authority; **and — new in revision 5 — that a `tier:` entry is a standing delegation whose membership changes without a commit, that the five legal tier names are GitHub's own, and that the tiers a project trusts are the project owner's decision and not an HOS default.** That last clause is the human's *"project-owner configurable per install, not a fixed HOS-wide list of trusted tiers"* expressed where a project owner will actually meet it. **HOS ships zero trusted tiers**, exactly as it ships zero trusted logins; the `.example` file shows the form without endorsing a value.

**`load_trusted_set(repo_root=".", collaborators=None) -> TrustedSet`** — composes the loaders. This is the only constructor production code uses. **The `collaborators` parameter is new in revision 5 and its shape is the whole of the design's answer to "the roster is now resolved over the network":** it takes an **already-fetched** collaborator payload (or `None`), exactly as `verify_codeowner_actor` takes an already-fetched events list. **`load_trusted_set` performs no network I/O**, so `requester_trust.py` remains testable with no stub, and **AD-1's no-network-inside-the-predicate rule holds unchanged.** The caller fetches (§1.3.2) and passes data in — the ADR-035 AD-2 shape this design already uses twice. **`collaborators=None` means "no tier resolution was performed", which yields `tier_members = frozenset()`**, which is the same fail-closed value as a failed resolution and is why `probe.py` — which does not fetch collaborators — needs no change to keep working.

#### 1.3.2 The permission-tier resolver (H3, binding — new in revision 5)

The human's ruling is that tier *membership* is *"resolved dynamically against the live GitHub permission API rather than requiring the roster to be hand-kept in sync with GitHub's own collaborator list"*, and that *"the committed roster records the policy … tier membership can change without a new commit, which is acceptable since the reviewable artifact is the policy decision, not the individual names."* **That is a requirements ruling and I implement it; what follows is the contract, and the parts that are mine are the fetch's placement, its bound, its failure direction and its cost.**

**`fetch_collaborators(repo: str) -> Any`** — a **fetch wrapper**, in the same class as `probe.py`'s events fetch and for the same reason: it is where the network lives, so the predicate has none.

| Property | Binding requirement |
|---|---|
| **Endpoint** | `gh api --paginate "repos/<repo>/collaborators?per_page=100"`. **One query per cycle for the whole gate**, not one per candidate — the result is a login→tier map consulted in memory. This is the single most important cost property of the feature and §6.1 asserts the call count |
| **Bound** | **`COLLABORATOR_PAGE_BOUND = 3`** (300 collaborators), a module constant and **not a flag**, for the same reason `LIST_PAGE_BOUND` is not (§2.2). Concatenated page arrays are normalised with the `re.sub(r"\]\s*\[", ",", out)` idiom §1.4 already specifies. **Exhausting the bound is not an error**: resolution proceeds over what was fetched and the gate emits `WARN collaborator-page-bound-reached pages=3`. It is completeness-affecting and can only under-trust |
| **When it runs** | **Lazily, and only if the roster lists at least one tier token.** `policy.tiers` empty → **the fetch is not issued at all and costs zero requests.** This is not an optimisation, it is the property that keeps the feature free for every deployment that does not use it — including this one, whose roster ships empty |
| **The field** | Membership is matched against each collaborator record's **`role_name`**, compared case-insensitively against the roster's tier tokens. **NOT the `permissions` object**, whose keys are a different vocabulary (`pull`/`triage`/`push`/`maintain`/`admin`) and which would silently fail every `read` and `write` entry. **§0.6 gap 8 records that I have not observed this payload**, and §6.1 carries the named acceptance check |
| **Matching is EXACT, not hierarchical — RULED BY THE HUMAN in revision 6; NO MECHANICAL CHANGE** | `tier:write` admits collaborators whose `role_name` is exactly `write` — **not** `maintain` and **not** `admin`. A policy that wants "write and above" lists all three. ~~**This is a deliberate choice and it is mine**~~ — **it was mine through revision 5 and it is the human's as of 2026-09-16T19:55:45Z.** The panel raised it three times independently (TP-5) and referred it as *"a one-sentence product ruling the human has not been asked"*; the human ruled: *"Keep the design as originally scoped: `tier:write` means exactly `write`, not `write`-and-above… **minimize the difference between the project owner's mental model and the system's actual behavior**… If someone wants admins/maintainers included, they add those roster entries explicitly. **The design already fails closed either way, so this is a UX/predictability choice, not a safety one.**"* **My original reasoning is retained because the ruling endorses it rather than replacing it:** inferring a hierarchy would mean the roster's meaning depends on a permission lattice written down nowhere in this repo; exact matching is narrower, fail-closed, and **legible from the file alone**, which is precisely the mental-model property the human named. The cost — a project owner who writes `tier:write` and expects their admins included — **is accepted by the human as a UX cost, and the `.example` file's header must still say so in terms (§4.3)**, because a ruled surprise is still a surprise |
| **Bot exclusion** | Every resolved login is passed through the same `is_bot_reviewer(login, record_type, bots)` guard the explicit-login form gets. **A GitHub App installed on the repo can appear in the collaborator list**, and a tier entry must not become the route around AD-2 that the roster's `[bot]` guard exists to close. This guard is applied to what the API returns, which is the only place it can be applied for a tier |
| **Failure direction** | **Any failure — non-zero exit, non-JSON, not a list, rate limit, timeout — yields `tier_members = frozenset()` and the gate CONTINUES.** It is **completeness-affecting, never security-affecting**: an empty resolution can only ever reduce who is trusted, and a record that would have been trusted is instead put through the full authorization check. Under **AM-31's criterion that is exit-0 class, not exit-2 class** |
| **But it is NOT silent, and it is NOT "complete"** | The gate emits `WARN tier-resolution-failed tiers=<n>` **and the determination is DEGRADED** (§2.2's exit **3**), because the emitted set may be a strict subset of what a healthy run would emit and the caller must not read a short answer as a statement about the queue. **This is the distinction §8/E-9 is about**: fail-closed-and-loud, not fail-closed-and-quiet |
| **Cost** | **0 requests when no tier is listed; 1–3 requests per cycle when one is.** Counted against §2.2.1's per-cycle request ceiling like every other request, which is the point of denominating that ceiling in requests (AM-30) rather than in checks |
| **Never cached** | No file, no memo, no TTL (FR7/AD-4). Every cycle re-resolves. **A cached tier membership is a cached trust determination** |

> **THE LIVE, SHIPPED INSTANCE OF THE VOCABULARY TRAP THIS SECTION REASONS ABOUT — recorded in revision 6, PRE-EXISTING, OUT OF S2'S SCOPE, and NOT FILED BY ME (TP-5).** The panel's completeness lens found it and I confirmed it at the line this session: **`scripts/framework/provision_agent_account.sh:190`** calls the *per-user* sibling of this design's endpoint — `repos/<slug>/collaborators/<user>/permission` — and reads **`--jq .permission`**, which is GitHub's **legacy** vocabulary. That field returns only `admin`/`write`/`read`/`none`, mapping `maintain`→`write` and `triage`→`read`. **Its `case` arms at `:200` (`worker-maintain`) and `:203` (`overseer-maintain`) can therefore never fire.** The failure is silent and benign — the script warns or confirms on a coarser grade than it thinks it is reading — but it is **exactly the trap this table's "The field" row exists to avoid**, shipped, today, in the same directory S1 lands in.
>
> **Three things follow, and the third is the one that changes a claim elsewhere in this document.** (1) **It is not S2's to fix.** `provision_agent_account.sh` is not on S2's change set (§4.3) and adding it would widen a `priority:critical` slice's blast radius for an unrelated defect. (2) **No issue appears to track it.** I searched this repository's open issues through the sanctioned path (`bootstrap/query_issues.sh --app worker --list --state open`) for `permission`, `collaborator`, `role_name` and `provision`; the only hit is **#1588**, which is a different defect (a branch-protection 403 crash in `check_required_content_checks()`). **That listing is page-capped at 100 with no pagination — TP-11's own finding — so the honest statement is "no issue found", not "no issue exists".** **I have not filed one**: filing is a worker action and this task is design-document-only. **The absence is recorded here so that it is a visible omission rather than a silent one**, in the same form §7.3 records the `startup-artifact-gap` issue's. (3) **It corrects §0.6 gap 8 and §9.5.** Both say *"nothing in the tree calls the endpoint"*. **That is wrong as written and the panel is right to say so:** nothing calls the **list** endpoint this design uses; the **per-user** one is called. **The `role_name` field remains unobserved on a live payload from this installation, which is the claim that actually matters** — and the fact that the one live caller reads the *wrong* vocabulary is the strongest available evidence that §0.6 gap 8 is not theoretical.

#### 1.3.1 The per-loader fail-closed contract, written down (AM-28(e), binding — new in revision 4)

Revision 3 specified *"missing file → empty set"* for `load_trusted_apps`, said **nothing at all** for `load_bot_accounts`, and left the rest to be inferred from §3's matrix. The panel found that F4 implies an unreadable `machine-accounts.env` collapses to "empty" while F3 and F7 make an unreadable CODEOWNERS or roster **propagate** — two opposite dispositions for the same class of failure, neither of them stated in §1.3. **Both directions are fail-closed and the contract is simply not written down.** It is written down here, in the same form §3 uses, because a contract a coder has to derive from a test matrix is a contract a coder will derive wrong once.

**The governing rule, stated first so the table reads as an application of it rather than a list of exceptions:** *"the file is absent"* and *"the file is present and I could not read it"* are **different facts and must never collapse into one another.** Absence is a configuration state the design supports; unreadability is a configuration state the design cannot reason about. The loaders express that as: absence → the **narrowest** value (empty, which can only ever reduce who is trusted); unreadability → **propagate the exception** and let the caller decide, which at the gate means exit 2 (§2.3 Step B).

| Loader | File absent (`FileNotFoundError`) | File present, read fails (any other `OSError`) | Present but malformed | Why this direction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `codeowners_humans` | **empty set**, no exception | **propagate** → gate exit 2 `config-error` (F3) | Unparseable lines are skipped by the existing field rules; a file yielding only `org/team` tokens is a legitimate empty result | The primitive stays permissive-of-absence for `probe.py` compatibility; the **gate** enforces non-empty at Step B2 (F1/F2). The asymmetry is deliberate and is stated in both places. |
| `load_trusted_apps` | **empty set**, no exception | **propagate** → gate exit 2 `config-error` | A present file with none of the three variables → empty set, which the gate rejects at Step B3 (F4) | Empty cannot widen. An unreadable file is not evidence of an empty trusted-app set. |
| `load_bot_accounts` | **empty set**, no exception — **and then `BOT_ACCOUNTS` may still union in** | **propagate** → gate exit 2 `config-error` | Present with no bot variables → empty baseline; env union still applies | **This is the row revision 3 omitted entirely, and it is the most dangerous one to get wrong**, because `bots` is the *exclusion* input: an empty denylist promotes every bot to "human". The gate therefore rejects an empty result at Step B1 (F4) **after** the env union, not before — so a file-absent-but-env-populated environment is still rejected if the union is empty, and an unreadable file never silently becomes "no bots exist". |
| `load_trusted_requesters` | **empty policy** (no logins, no tiers), no exception (F6 — FR3 requires the mechanism to function with CODEOWNERS + apps alone) | **propagate** → gate exit 2 `roster-unreadable` (F7) | Malformed line → **skipped with a stderr diagnostic naming the line number** (F8); `[bot]` login → **rejected with a diagnostic** (F9); **unrecognised tier name → skipped with a diagnostic listing the five legal values (F34)** | A malformed roster grants nothing but must not stop the repo. An **unreadable** one is different: it may contain entries, and proceeding as though it were empty would silently narrow a trust boundary a human curated — which fails closed on trust but fails *silently* on intent, and §6.1 pins the diagnostic. |
