| **`fetch_collaborators` / tier resolution (NEW, H3)** | n/a — it is a network read, not a file. **Not issued at all when `policy.tiers` is empty** | **NEVER propagates.** Any failure → `tier_members = frozenset()`, `WARN tier-resolution-failed`, **exit 3 (degraded)**, walk continues (F35) | Page bound exhausted → resolve over what was fetched, `WARN collaborator-page-bound-reached`; a record with no `role_name`, or one not matching a listed tier, simply does not join the set | **This row is deliberately the only one in the table that does not propagate, and the asymmetry is the ruling rather than an exception to it.** The other four are *configuration*: a wrong trust, bot or CODEOWNER set changes **every** record's determination and can do so permissively, so AM-31 routes them to exit 2. Tier resolution is *membership*: its failure can only ever **shrink** the trusted set, which puts more records through the authorization check, not fewer. **It is completeness-affecting, so it reports and continues (AM-31); it is not silent, so the determination is degraded (AM-32).** Treating it like a config failure would hand any transient GitHub 5xx a repo-wide stop — which is precisely the RP-7 harm AM-31 exists to remove, re-created in a new place. |

**Two boundaries the loaders must honour, which follow from the table rather than being separate rules:**

- **No loader may catch a bare `Exception`.** Only `FileNotFoundError` is benign, and only at the four sites above. A blanket `except Exception: return set()` converts every row's right-hand column into its left-hand one at a stroke, turning three propagating failures into three silent empties — and for `load_bot_accounts` that is the promote-every-bot case. This is the single most consequential line a coder could write in `requester_trust.py`, and §6.1 tests all four propagation paths individually rather than as a group.
- **A diagnostic is required wherever a loader discards input it read.** F8 and F9 are the only two such cases today. A loader that silently drops a roster entry it parsed and rejected is a loader whose behaviour differs from its file, with nothing anywhere recording the difference.

*Traceability (AR-5):* this section implements **FR3** (per-entry provenance, enforced in the parser), **FR19** and **FR24/FR25** (fail-closed, anti-tamper; AR-6 marks all three in force on S2 merge). `load_bot_accounts`' union-only env rule implements **AD-13** and is the subject of F5.

### 1.4 Predicates

**`is_trusted_requester(login, user_type, trusted_set) -> tuple[bool, str]`** — the ADR-bound signature (AD-1). **Pure: no network, no filesystem, no environment.** Evaluation order is fixed and total:

1. `login` empty/absent → `(False, "no-login")`.
2. `low = login.lower()`.
3. If `low in trusted_set.codeowners` **or** `low in trusted_set.roster` **or `low in trusted_set.tier_members`**:
   - if `is_bot_reviewer(login, user_type, trusted_set.bots)` → `(False, "bot-in-human-category")`;
   - else → `(True, "codeowner")`, `(True, "roster")`, or **`(True, "roster-tier:<tier>")`** respectively, **checked in that order**.
4. If `low in trusted_set.apps` → `(True, "trusted-app")`.
5. Otherwise → `(False, "not-in-trusted-set")`.

**Step 3's third disjunct is new in revision 5 (the human's H3 ruling), and three properties of how it was added are load-bearing.** **(i) It is inside the SAME branch as the other two human categories, not beside it** — so it inherits the bot guard automatically rather than needing one written for it, and a future editor cannot add a fourth human category without meeting the guard. **(ii) The order is fixed and CODEOWNERS wins** — a login that is both a CODEOWNER and a `write` collaborator reports `codeowner`, because the more specific and more durable provenance is the more useful one in an audit. **(iii) The reason token names the tier that admitted them**, which requires resolving *which* of the roster's tiers matched; §1.3.2's resolver therefore returns a login→tier mapping and `tier_members` is its key set. **This still costs no I/O in the predicate** — the mapping is passed in with the `TrustedSet`. *(Revision 7, RP4-6(a): through revision 6 no `TrustedSet` field carried that mapping. `tier_members` is a bare `frozenset[str]`, so `roster-tier:<tier>` could not be computed as typed. §1.2 adds `tier_of: Mapping[str, str]` with the invariant `frozenset(tier_of) == tier_members`. Membership is still decided by `tier_members` alone, and `tier_of` only names the tier.)*

Step 3's bot guard is the requester-side twin of the shipped `test_bot_labeling_is_never_authorized_even_if_in_codeowners`: a bot login mislisted in CODEOWNERS, slipped past the roster parser, **or returned by the collaborators endpoint** must not confer requester trust either. Categories (a) and (c) are human categories by definition; the guard makes that structural rather than assumed. **The third case is not hypothetical: a GitHub App installed on a repository can appear in its collaborator list**, so without this guard a `tier:write` entry would be a route around AD-2 — exactly what the roster's own `[bot]` rejection exists to close, arriving through a door AD-1(c) did not have when that rejection was written.

**What step 3's third disjunct is NOT, stated because it is the most likely misreading of an override the human made against the architect's recommendation.** It is **not** a fourth membership category in AD-1 — it is a second *entry form* for AD-1(c), which is what the human's own note says: *"this ruling is scoped entirely to the requester-trust roster's own entry format, not to who may authorize."* It is **not** an authorization channel: `tier_members` is an input to this function and to nothing else, it is absent from `verify_codeowner_actor`'s argument list by construction, and **under AR-7 the only authorizing signal in the S2 era is a `labeled` event for the dispatch label whose actor is a verified individual human CODEOWNER.** A `write` collaborator is trusted *as a requester*, which means their issues need no authorizing act; it does **not** mean they can perform one on someone else's. §6.1 pins both halves.

**This function is not an eligibility test.** It answers set membership only. Step 4 returns `True` for *any* issue authored by a trusted app, including a laundered one — AD-2's narrowing is applied by `requester_verdict`, below. A conformance test (§6.1) asserts that `is_trusted_requester` has no production caller other than `requester_verdict`.

**`machine_filing_marker(title: str) -> MachineFilingMarker | None`** — pure. Returns the first table entry (§1.5) for which `title.startswith(entry.title_prefix)` and (`entry.title_contains is None or entry.title_contains in title`). Case-sensitive. Dormant entries match (a dormant emitting site that is re-enabled must not silently break).

**`requester_verdict(record: Mapping, trusted_set: TrustedSet) -> RequesterVerdict`** — the **only** composition any consumer may use to decide eligibility.

1. `user = record.get("user") or {}`; `login = user.get("login", "")`; `user_type = user.get("type", "")`.
2. `trusted, reason = is_trusted_requester(login, user_type, trusted_set)`.
3. If not `trusted` → `RequesterVerdict(False, reason, None, None)`.
4. If `reason == "trusted-app"` (AD-2 narrowing):
   - `m = machine_filing_marker(record.get("title") or "")`;
   - `m is None` → `RequesterVerdict(False, "trusted-app-no-machine-filing-marker", "trusted-app", None)`;
   - else → `RequesterVerdict(True, f"trusted-app:{m.marker_id}", "trusted-app", m.marker_id)`.
5. Else → `RequesterVerdict(True, reason, reason, None)`.

**Boundary the caller must honour:** `requester_verdict` reads only `record["user"]["login"]`, `record["user"]["type"]`, and `record["title"]`. It MUST NOT be passed a record whose fields were derived from anything but a GitHub API response (FR5). It never reads the body, and it never reads a label.

**`verify_codeowner_actor(events, codeowners_humans, bot_accounts, label_name) -> str | None`** — AD-4's live authorization test, promoted out of `probe.py` as a **pure function over an already-fetched events list** (AD-1: no network inside the predicate; the caller fetches and passes data in — the ADR-035 AD-2 shape). **The signature LOST a parameter in revision 5** — see the ruling below.

1. `events` not a list → `None`.
2. Build `relevant` in API order: `event == "labeled"` **and** `event.label.name == label_name` → append `event.actor`. **There is no second arm.**
3. For `actor` in `reversed(relevant)`: skip if no login; **if `is_bot_reviewer(login, actor.type, bot_accounts)` → `continue`**; if `login.lower() in codeowners_humans` → return `login`.
4. Return `None`.

**AR-7 / AM-35 — the `milestoned` arm and `expected_milestone_title` are DELETED, not sentinelled (binding).** `pm-agent`'s **AR-7** rules the property: *"A `milestoned` event MUST NOT authorize anything. An authorization is established by exactly one signal: a `labeled` event, for the dispatch label being queried, whose GitHub-reported actor is a verified individual human CODEOWNER."* **AM-35** rules the mechanics and chooses deletion over a retained-but-ignored parameter, for a reason worth carrying rather than paraphrasing: *"a sentinel leaves a live code path that a single call-site edit re-enables, and leaves the function's contract ambiguous to every reader. Deletion makes AR-7 a structural property rather than a convention."*

**Three things this deletion settles, each of which revision 4 spent a section on:**

- **It SIMPLIFIES the shipped call site rather than changing it.** `probe.py:428-431` passes no `expected_milestone_title` today (re-read this revision, §0.0.4) — S1 was going to *add* one. Now it does not. **The parameter is removed from a signature no production caller ever passed it to.**
- **AM-4's entire apparatus is RETIRED AS SPENT, not overturned, and the distinction is a ruling.** Byte-for-byte title equality, the inverted default, the em-dash/prefix-matching asymmetry against `bootstrap/edit_issue.sh:149-169`, F27's *"single most important test in §6.1"*, F28, F29 and residual **R-7** all tested or accepted the safety of a parameter **that no longer exists**. AM-4 was *correct* — the panel's 97/97 `{"title"}` measurement showed title equality was the only discriminator available — and it is retired because its subject is gone. **Retiring it must be visible as a ruling, or a reviewer reads F27's absence as an omission** (AM-35's own words).
- **It removes a whole hazard class rather than narrowing one.** FIND-1 (§0.2), R-7's milestone-rename de-authorization, R-3's *different-milestone* half, the unobserved `milestone` payload shape (§0.6 gap 5), and AM-18's milestone-object-absent skip with its new `probe.py` stderr surface were **all** consequences of one channel. **All of them go together.** That is the strongest available argument that AR-7 is the right narrowing, and it is a design observation rather than a ruling, so I state it here: *the second door on the vault did not merely have a weaker lock; it had five of this design's residuals hanging off it.*

**What is NOT changed by the deletion, stated because AM-35 names this as its most likely misreading:** **Step C still selects by milestone.** *"The milestone loses the power to authorize, not the power to scope. An implementer who removes the milestone from the list query has broken selection entirely while every authorization test still passes."* §2.3 Step C is unchanged.

Three properties that are deliberate and must survive review:

- **Order is exclusion-then-positive-membership.** `is_bot_reviewer` decides only "is this a bot"; CODEOWNERS membership is what confers authorization. Inverting them, or reading `not is_bot_reviewer(...)` as authorization, admits every anonymous member of the public (VF-5). A repo-wide conformance test (§6.1) asserts the literal `not is_bot_reviewer` appears nowhere in production code.
- **A bot actor is `continue`, not `return None`** — the shipped semantics (`probe.py:319-320`, re-read this revision). A bot relabelling after a CODEOWNER does not erase the CODEOWNER's act. This deliberately differs from `_verify_label_actor`'s "found the event, actor not allowed → None"; the difference is intended and is stated in the shared docstring, because a reviewer encountering both will otherwise read one as a bug. **It is also the mechanism of residual R-3**, which revision 5 restates for this channel (§7.2, AM-36) — and the docstring must say so **at the code site**, which is AM-35's last binding row.
- **The function is now single-channel, and that is the property to defend.** With one arm there is exactly one shape an authorization can have. Events for `unlabeled` are ignored — see residual **R-3** (§7.2), **accepted for S2 by AM-8, restated for the dispatch-label channel by AM-36, with #1542 named as its live instance.** AM-8's S3 clearing rule is stated in §7.2 in full so it is not redesigned from scratch, **but note the human's H1 item 3 ruling: S5 is deferred indefinitely and no digest-binding fix is being built, so R-3's closure now has no scheduled date and §7.2 says so rather than implying one.**

**The `probe.py` docstring correction is IN SCOPE for this change set (AM-35, binding).** `probe.py:292-294` says the function *"checks the most recent 'labeled' event … and the most recent 'milestoned' event"*. Verified at the line this revision: it does neither — `:315-322` walks the **whole** event list backwards and `continue`s past bot actors. `pm-agent` declined to place the fix; the architect places it in scope on three grounds, the middle one measured rather than argued: the function is being edited in this change set anyway, so a docstring describing a **deleted** channel would be strictly worse than today's error; **the error misled a cross-vendor adversarial panel into measuring the wrong query** (`REQUIREMENTS-1540-AMENDMENT-2` §0.1 Correction 2); and AM-28(c) already ruled that wrong citations in this chain are *"not cosmetic."* **Binding: the corrected docstring states the actual rule — *any* `labeled` event for the dispatch label, at *any* point in the issue's history, whose actor is a verified individual human CODEOWNER — and names residual R-3 as its consequence, at the code site.**

**Bounded pagination (FIND-2 / AM-5, binding).** The *fetch wrappers* — never this pure function; AD-1's no-network rule holds and is what makes the predicate testable without a stub — request `--paginate` with `per_page=100`, bounded to **10 pages (1000 events)**, and normalise `gh --paginate`'s concatenated page arrays with the `re.sub(r"\]\s*\[", ",", out)` idiom already present at `scripts/framework/require_human_approval.py:130-138` (re-read this revision; its own comment records that the boundary is `"]\n["` or `"] ["`, not a literal `"]["`, which is why a naive `replace("][", ",")` is wrong). Three rules on the bound:

1. **Exhausting the bound without a match is "not authorized / skip that candidate", never an error.** It is fail-closed for exactly that issue and leaves every other candidate unaffected. **It is a DETERMINATION** — the gate looked and found nothing — so it counts toward `gated`.
2. **A transport failure QUARANTINES the record and the walk CONTINUES — CHANGED IN REVISION 5 (AM-31, which REPLACES AM-21).** Revision 4 exited 2 at the gate. It now counts the record under `unevaluated:query-failed` with its issue number, never as `gated`, and moves on; `probe.py` still returns `None` (unchanged, and `probe.py:298-303` is where that behaviour already ships). **"The query failed" and "the query succeeded and found nothing" remain different facts and must not collapse** — which is exactly why one is `unevaluated` and the other is `gated`. §2.3 D5.3 carries the full reasoning and §8/E-9 records how the exit code expresses it.
3. **Hitting the bound MUST emit a stderr diagnostic naming the issue number** — e.g. `select_work_candidates: WARN events-page-bound-reached issue=#<n> pages=10`. Without it, R-5 is an invisible permanent stall on precisely this repo's most-relabelled issues, which are also its most important ones.
4. **The bound is additionally clamped by the remaining request budget — NEW in revision 5 (AM-30).** A record's events fetch may consume at most `min(EVENTS_PAGE_BOUND, effective_ceiling - api_requests)` pages, so the per-cycle **request** ceiling can never be exceeded by a fetch already in flight. ~~If the clamp bites before a match is found, the outcome is identical to rule 1 — gated, `WARN events-page-bound-reached` — plus `WARN budget-clamped-events-fetch issue=#<n>`.~~ **This is what makes AM-30's ceiling a ceiling rather than a target**; §2.2.1 defines the counter.

   **REPLACED IN REVISION 7 (run 3's TP-2, reinstated by RUN4 RP4-3; RUN4 RP4-1(i)): a clamp that bites is NOT identical to rule 1. It is `unevaluated:cost-ceiling`, it is the ceiling stop, and the walk ends there.** Binding, in four parts:
   - **(a) When the clamp BITES.** The clamp limit for a record is `L = effective_ceiling − api_requests` pages, taken when that record's fetch begins. The clamp bites iff **all four** hold: `L < EVENTS_PAGE_BOUND`; the fetch consumed all `L` pages; unfetched pages remained, as GitHub's `Link` header indicates (`rel="next"` when walking oldest-first; the unvisited end when using the permitted newest-end walk below); and no qualifying actor was found in the pages fetched. **If the gate cannot tell whether a further page exists, it MUST treat the clamp as having bitten.** "Unevaluated" is the outcome that never makes a false claim about a person's queue. A fetch that ends because the event history ended is a complete fetch whatever `L` was, and its no-match outcome is a determination: `no-codeowner-actor`, gated (rule 1's reasoning applies to it exactly). `L = 0` never reaches a fetch, because §2.2.1's stop test refuses the first request; that is the ordinary ceiling stop at this record.
   - **(b) What a bite produces.** The record is counted under `unevaluated:cost-ceiling`, **never** `gated`, and named on its own line, `select_work_candidates: unevaluated:cost-ceiling issue=#<n>` (routine class, no `WARN` prefix; §1.2 consequence 7). `WARN budget-clamped-events-fetch issue=#<n>` (F14b) still fires as the operator's diagnostic of *why* this record was cut short. **`WARN events-page-bound-reached` does NOT fire.** That line names a determination (rule 3, F14), and a bite is the absence of one.
   - **(c) A bite IS the ceiling stop.** After a bite, `api_requests == effective_ceiling` by construction, and **the walk ends COMPLETELY**: no further record is admitted, **trusted or otherwise**, even though a trusted-authored record would cost nothing. Without this clause the walk would step over a record it could not determine and then admit a free record ranked below it. That is exactly RUN4's counterexample 1 (`#30` clamped, `#20` trusted admitted, `#10` never reached), which made the admission property false and reported a record a complete walk authorizes as *"held for human authorization"*.
   - **(d) A clamped fetch that FINDS a match is a determination: eligible.** This is sound because `verify_codeowner_actor` is monotone in its input. It walks every `labeled` event and ignores `unlabeled` (R-3), so a qualifying actor on any subset of the history is a qualifying actor on the whole. **One precision for the `AUTHORIZED … actor=<login>` line:** it names the most recent qualifying actor **within the pages fetched**. On the default oldest-first walk, a later qualifying actor on an unfetched page is not named. Eligibility is unaffected. This was already true of rule 1's page-bound case and is stated here because the clamp makes it reachable at low budgets.

   **Why this reopens nothing.** The clamp is this document's own revision-5 construction. Amendment 4 does not mention it (re-read this session). **AM-5 (page bound exhausted → gated) is untouched**, because rule 1 still governs a fetch that reached `EVENTS_PAGE_BOUND` or the end of the history. What is applied is **AM-20** (*"a record the walk never reached is unevaluated"*, and `gated` counts determinations only) and **AM-30** (the ceiling is a ceiling).

**Permitted, not required:** walking pages from the newest end via the `Link: rel="last"` cursor, which would make the bound effectively unreachable. If the coder does this, the pure function's contract is unchanged and the events list passed in must still be in API order (oldest-first), because step 3 walks it `reversed`.

### 1.5 AD-2 — the machine-filing marker: enumeration, mechanism, and its honest limits

#### 1.5.1 The real enumeration

The ADR named "at least three" sites. The exhaustive search this session covered: every caller of `bootstrap/create_issue.sh`; every `gh issue create` in `bin/`, `scripts/`, `bootstrap/`; every `--method POST` against `/issues` in `scripts/**/*.py`; and `.github/workflows/**`. The complete set is **seven emitting sites in three classes**, plus one dormant:

| # | Site | Title | Labels | Milestone | Reaches the candidate set? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `bin/hos-cron:959` | `[BLOCKED] agent unavailable — <role> halted (missing: …)` | `needs-human,needs-ai` | none | No — `needs-human` excluded, no milestone |
| 2 | `bin/hos-cron:1414` (`_dm_title_prefix`) | `[BLOCKED] local main diverged on <project> (<role>) — needs manual recovery` | `needs-human,priority:critical` | none | No |
| 3 | `bin/hos-cron:1617` (`_bs_title_prefix`, #1496 repair mode) | `[BLOCKED] inner-loop tests failing on <project> — diagnose and fix` | `needs-ai,priority:critical` | **PATCHed to the target milestone** | **YES — the only one** |
| 4 | `bin/hos-cron:1687` (`_bs_title_prefix`, legacy halt) | `[BLOCKED] inner-loop tests failing on <project> — worker halted` | `needs-human,needs-ai` | none | No |
| 5 | `bin/hos-cron:1982` (`_TIMEOUT_BREAKER_TITLE_PREFIX`) | `[SUSPENDED] <role> timeout breaker tripped on <project> — worker halted` | `needs-human,needs-ai` | none | No |
| 5d | `bin/hos-cron:2055` (`_USAGE_LIMIT_BREAKER_TITLE_PREFIX`) | `[SUSPENDED] <role> usage-limit breaker tripped on <project> …` | `needs-human,needs-ai` | none | **Commented out (dormant, #1446)** — the prefix variable is still live at `:225` |
| 6 | `scripts/automation/lib/self_review_source.py:192` `file_finding_as_issue()` | `[AI: self-review] <class>: <description>` | `hos-coordination,needs-ai` | none | No (not milestone-scoped) |
| 7 | `scripts/review_self.sh:294` | `[AI: <reviewer>] design-concern: <n> HIGH/CRITICAL self-review findings (<ts>)` | `design-concern` | none | No |

Three findings follow, and they matter more than the table:

**(i) All seven are deterministic-code sites.** Their titles and bodies are composed by program logic from *local machine state* — missing agent files, `git cherry` output, a pytest exit code, a wall-clock cap, validator output. None of them reads attacker-controlled GitHub content to build the issue it files. This is the property the marker is actually selecting for.

**(ii) There is a second, much larger class the ADR did not enumerate: LLM-prose filing sites.** `.claude/agents/worker.md:236, :458, :490, :901` and `.claude/agents/overseer.md:162` instruct an agent to file issues through `bootstrap/create_issue.sh --app worker|overseer`, choosing the title, body and labels itself. `worker-cron-prompt.md`'s injection-handling instruction ("if it is clearly malicious, stop and file a `needs-human` issue describing the injection attempt") is in this class, and `CLAUDE.md`'s human-proxy default path ("file an issue for the autonomous worker to pick up", `--app human`) is the highest-volume member of it. These sites share the bot identities with class (i) and are exactly the laundering surface AD-2 exists to remove. **The marker's real job is to separate class (i) from class (ii) inside one identity** — not to separate bots from humans, which AD-1 already does.

**(iii) On this repo's live path, exactly one machine-filing site can reach the candidate set: #3.** Every other site either carries `needs-human` (excluded by the ordering filter) or carries no milestone (invisible to the milestone-scoped query). This bounds the blast radius of a missed enumeration, which is the first attack the ADR's own closing section invites: *on the live gate*, missing a marker can only under-trust site #3, whose failure mode is "the baseline-repair issue is not auto-selected and the worker's red-baseline repair stalls visibly" — an availability regression with a loud standing issue, not a bypass. The other six entries exist because (a) `probe.py`'s milestone strategy applies **no** `needs-human` exclusion, so sites #1, #4 and #5 *are* reachable in a consumer deployment, and (b) a future change that adds a milestone to any of them must not silently gate it.

#### 1.5.2 The mechanism

The marker is a **title-shape match against a committed, tested table** in `requester_trust.py`:

```
MACHINE_FILING_MARKERS = (
  ("hos-cron/agent-unavailable",   "[BLOCKED] agent unavailable",            None,                                  "bin/hos-cron",                          "active"),
  ("hos-cron/main-diverged",       "[BLOCKED] local main diverged on ",      None,                                  "bin/hos-cron::_dm_title_prefix",        "active"),
  ("hos-cron/baseline-red",        "[BLOCKED] inner-loop tests failing on ", None,                                  "bin/hos-cron::_bs_title_prefix",        "active"),
  ("hos-cron/timeout-breaker",     "[SUSPENDED] ",                           " timeout breaker tripped on ",        "bin/hos-cron::_TIMEOUT_BREAKER_TITLE_PREFIX",     "active"),
  ("hos-cron/usage-limit-breaker", "[SUSPENDED] ",                           " usage-limit breaker tripped on ",    "bin/hos-cron::_USAGE_LIMIT_BREAKER_TITLE_PREFIX", "dormant"),
  ("self-review/finding",          "[AI: self-review] ",                     None,                                  "scripts/automation/lib/self_review_source.py::file_finding_as_issue", "active"),
  ("review-self/design-concern",   "[AI: ",                                  " design-concern: ",                   "scripts/review_self.sh",                "active"),
)
```

**Where the marker is written:** nowhere new. It is the title string the deterministic sites already emit. This is deliberate — see D-2 below.

**How the gate verifies it:** `requester_verdict` consults the table **only after** `is_trusted_requester` has already returned `"trusted-app"`, i.e. only after GitHub has reported the author as one of the three HOS App identities.

**Why a non-bot author cannot forge it:** because the marker is never a trust source. A stranger may title their issue `[BLOCKED] agent unavailable — please fix` and it changes nothing: their `issue.user.login` is not in `TrustedSet.apps`, so step 4 of `is_trusted_requester` is never reached and the marker is never consulted. The marker is a **one-way narrowing filter applied inside an already-identity-confirmed set** — exactly the direction AD-2 and FR24 permit. It can remove trust; it can never add it. §6.2 names this as its own test case.

**Design decisions, with reasons:**

- **D-1 — the marker is the title, not a body token or an HTML comment.** The title is already in the list response the gate fetches, so verification costs **zero additional API calls** (AD-3's cost note holds). A body marker would require either `per_page`-inflated payloads or a per-issue fetch, which would put an API call on the *trusted* path — the one path the ADR's cost argument depends on staying free.
- **D-2 — no new marker is stamped into the emitting sites.** Five of the seven are `gh issue create` calls in `bin/hos-cron`, a protected surface; adding a body token there would be five protected-surface edits buying nothing. More importantly, a marker that *every* invocation of a wrapper emits is **isomorphic to trusting the identity** — it carries zero information and would reinstate the laundering path under a new name. A marker is only meaningful when the set of call sites that can emit it is a strict subset of the set of call sites the identity can reach. That holds for the worker/overseer Apps (class (i) ⊂ class (i)+(ii)); it does **not** hold for the human-proxy App, every one of whose filings is class (ii) — which is precisely why §8/E-1 cannot be resolved with a marker.
- **D-3 — the table is enforced by a conformance test, not by review.** For each entry, a test asserts the literal `title_prefix` (and `title_contains`) appears in the named emitting file. A second test counts issue-creation call sites — `grep -c "gh issue create" bin/hos-cron == 6` (5 active + 1 commented), `scripts/review_self.sh == 1`, and exactly one `--method POST` to `/issues` in `scripts/automation/lib/` — so adding an eighth deterministic filing site fails the build until the enumeration is updated. This is the mechanical answer to "if a machine-filing site is missed, a live path breaks."
- **D-4 — the honest limit.** Within a single bot identity **no in-band marker can be unforgeable against induction**, because the inducing agent has the same write authority and can read the marker table out of the repo. The marker removes the large, easily-reached laundering surface (any LLM-composed bot filing) and leaves a small, specific one (an LLM induced into reproducing a deterministic template byte-for-byte, *and* into applying a milestone, *and* into applying `needs-ai`). The only known unforgeable construction is **a separate GitHub App identity used exclusively by deterministic filing code**, which is structural and out of S1/S2's scope. **Ruled by AM-7: declined for now**, with R-4 accepted as a recorded residual and three named revisit triggers — see D-6.

- **D-5 — no creation-time assertion may ever confer trust (AM-2, binding; this is AD-13 as clarified).** Nothing passed at *issue-creation* time — no flag, no argument, no body token, no HTML marker, no envelope, no "the human confirmed this" convention documented alongside a tool — may cause the resulting issue to be treated as trusted or approved, **under any identity, with any human-confirmation convention attached to it**. Authorization is only ever derived live, at selection time, from a GitHub-reported act by a verified human CODEOWNER (AD-4). The reason is not fastidiousness: *a creation-time assertion is a claim about a fact the reader cannot check.* At the moment the gate reads it there is no human present, no way to distinguish a flag an attentive human asked for from a flag an induced agent typed, and no record of which it was.

  This is what permanently forecloses §8/E-1's **option B** (a `--confirmed` flag on `create_issue.sh --app human`). The `submit_pr.sh --app human --confirmed` precedent **does not transfer**, and the distinction is worth stating because it will be proposed again: there, the flag gates *a mutation the human is watching happen*, and it confers no standing authority on any later autonomous decision. Here it would confer autonomous-build authorization on a future cycle, read by a machine, with no human present at read time. Option **A** (stamping a marker on `--app human` filings) is rejected as **vacuous by the subset argument** — the architect adopted that argument as binding reasoning and credited it to this document, so it is now a rule rather than my opinion: *a marker is meaningful only when the set of call sites that can emit it is a strict subset of the set of call sites the identity can reach.* For the human-proxy App every filing is LLM-composed, so the emitting set equals the reachable set and the predicate carries zero bits.

- **D-6 — R-4's three revisit triggers, and the tests that make them detectable (AM-7, binding).** Any one of these reopens the separate-App question (§8/E-2) as a **required decision, not an option**:

  1. the assessment layer (S5) ever gains authorization-relevant authority — AD-13 says it must not, so this trigger is a tripwire on AD-13 itself;
  2. **any entry in `MACHINE_FILING_MARKERS` acquires an LLM-composed emitting site** — in which case that entry MUST be removed from the table **in the same change**, because its subset property has gone;
  3. a second machine-filing site becomes reachable by the milestone-scoped candidate query (today exactly one is — §1.5.1(iii)).

  §6.1's `test_marker_table_literals_exist_in_emitting_files` and `test_issue_creation_site_count_is_pinned` are the mechanism that makes triggers 2 and 3 detectable rather than noticed-later, and they are **binding, not optional**. A reviewer tempted to relax either as "brittle string tests" should read them as the enforcement arm of a declined structural change.

- **D-7 — sub-issue filing (`ADR-1604` AD-9) MUST NOT be added to this table (AM-10, binding).** The architect found a contradiction inside `ADR-1604` AD-9 that revision 1 did not have: its point 1 says sub-issues "never acquire authorization by authorship" and are authorized because the parent was; its point 2 said sub-issue filing is an enumerated machine-filing path under AD-2. **Point 1 governs; point 2 is amended.** A sub-issue's title and body are composed by an LLM from the parent's content, so it is squarely class (ii) — a marker on it would be exactly as vacuous as one on the human-proxy App, and worse, its content derives from the very issue whose trust is in question. Its authorization is an **AD-4-class live derivation**: at selection time the gate resolves the parent from the machine-readable derivation link and requires the parent to be **authorized now**, by the same test — never cached (FR7), never inherited from a determination made when the sub-issue was filed. Designing that check belongs to #1604's chain and it is a **new consumer of AD-1/AD-4's primitive, not a second trust definition** (#1135). S1/S2's enumeration is complete as of today and does not anticipate it; the table must simply stay closed against it.

### 1.6 `probe.py` refactor (AD-1: refactored onto, never copied from — #1135)

> **REVISION 5 SHRINKS THIS SECTION, and the shrinkage is the finding rather than a tidy-up.** Revision 4 asked S1 to *add* a parameter, *add* a milestone-object skip branch, *add* a new stderr output surface to a module that has none, and *change six existing tests*. **AR-7 and AM-35 remove all four.** What remains is what §1.6's own heading has always claimed it was: a refactor that promotes two functions and changes no behaviour except by subtraction. **A reviewer who remembers revision 4's version of this section should read the difference as S1's blast radius falling, not as work being quietly dropped** — every retirement below is named, with the ruling that retired it.

| Before | After |
|---|---|
| `def _codeowners_humans(repo_root=".") -> set[str]:` (`probe.py:257-277`, 21 lines) | `from scripts.framework.requester_trust import codeowners_humans as _codeowners_humans` — a re-export alias, zero logic |
| `def _verify_codeowner_actor(owner, repo, issue_number, label_name, codeowners_humans, bot_accounts) -> Optional[str]:` (`probe.py:280-324` — fetch + decide, 45 lines) | **Signature UNCHANGED** (revision 4 added `expected_milestone_title`; **AM-35 deletes it before it is ever added**). Body reduced to: fetch events via `_run_gh` with bounded `--paginate`, then `return requester_trust.verify_codeowner_actor(events, …)`. On `GitHubError` → `None` (**unchanged, and `probe.py:298-303` is the shipped quarantine AM-31 restores to the gate**). **The `elif ev == "milestoned"` arm at `:312-313` is DELETED.** **The docstring at `:288-297` is CORRECTED** — AM-35, §1.4 |
| `bot_accounts` default `os.environ["BOT_ACCOUNTS"].split()` | `requester_trust.load_bot_accounts(repo_root)` — file baseline ∪ env. Strictly stricter; when the env is set as today, identical. |
| `probe_repo` milestone branch calls `_verify_codeowner_actor(owner, repo, n, "needs-ai", codeowners_humans, bots)` (**`probe.py:428-431`** — line range **corrected in revision 5**; revisions 2–4 and Amendment 4 all cite `:427-431`, and `:427` is the second line of the preceding comment) | **UNCHANGED.** Revision 4 was going to add `expected_milestone_title=issue["milestone"]["title"]` and a milestone-object skip; **AM-35 retires both.** The call site is not edited at all |
| *(revision 4: a new `sys` import and a stderr write)* | **NOT ADDED.** `probe.py` writes nothing to stderr and does not import `sys` (verified this revision, §0.0.4), and **the only reason S1 needed that surface was AM-18's skip diagnostic, which AM-35 retires.** The coder's obligation to check whether `multi_customer.py` captures `probe.py`'s stderr **goes with it** |
| *(revision 4: `TrustedSet` construction)* | **`probe.py` does not construct a `TrustedSet` and does not resolve permission tiers.** H3's tier machinery is the gate's; `probe.py` calls `codeowners_humans` and `load_bot_accounts` directly, exactly as it does today. **`load_trusted_set(collaborators=None)` is why this needs no change** (§1.3) |

**The milestone-less skip and its diagnostic are RETIRED (AM-35, binding — and the retirement, not the rule, is what a reader needs to inherit).** Revision 2 applied the skip under objection (§8/E-7); Amendment 2's **AM-18** answered the objection with three reasons, the third decisive, and I withdrew it. **AM-35 now retires AM-18 in full — the skip, the `WARN milestone-object-absent` diagnostic, §1.6's diagnostic contract table, TD row F17, and `probe.py`'s new stderr surface all go.** The architect's ground is exact and is quoted rather than summarised, because the *reason* is what stops this being re-added:

> *"AM-18's stated purpose was 'never authorize blind, and never fall back to the labeled channel.' With no milestone channel, the milestone object is never read for authorization and there is nothing to fall back from. Retaining it would ship a fail-closed branch guarding a decision that no longer exists, plus a new stderr surface in `probe.py` (a module that writes no stderr today) for no remaining purpose. This also retires `probe.py`'s new stderr output surface entirely, which is a real simplification of S1."*

**Read AM-18's third reason against AR-7 and the retirement follows mechanically rather than by judgement.** AM-18's decisive argument was that *"the skip rule and AM-4's derive-the-title-from-the-record construction are two halves of one thing"* — the expected title came from `issue["milestone"]["title"]`, which was sound only because the server-side filter guaranteed the record's milestone *was* the milestone under consideration, and an absent milestone object was exactly that guarantee failing. **AM-4's construction is gone, so its other half has nothing to be a half of.** The pairing that made the skip coherent is the same pairing that makes its removal obligatory. **Keeping the skip now would be the inconsistent design AM-18 warned against, with the signs reversed.**

**Three consequences, each of which a reviewer would otherwise have to reconstruct:**

1. **`probe.py` gains no output surface and the coder gains no obligation to audit `multi_customer.py`'s stderr handling.** That obligation existed only to protect the diagnostic (§0.0.2, verified again this revision).
2. **E-7's withdrawal stands and is NOT reopened.** I objected; AM-18 answered; I withdrew on the reasoning. **AM-35 does not vindicate the objection** — the skip was correct for as long as AM-4 existed — and §8/E-7 is annotated rather than re-argued. *"Moot is not the same as wrong"* applies to my own objection as much as to the panel's measurement.
3. **The invariant itself is still true and is still worth knowing**: Step C queries `&milestone=<n>` server-side, so every record carries a milestone object by construction. **The gate simply no longer has any reason to look**, because nothing it decides reads the field. An invariant with no consumer needs no guard.

**Compatibility contract for the coder — the import surface holds; the "no test changes" claim does not (AM-4).** `tests/automation/test_probe.py` patches `scripts.automation.lib.probe._run_gh` and imports `_codeowners_humans` / `_verify_codeowner_actor` from `probe`. Both names MUST remain importable from `probe` and the fetch MUST continue to go through `probe._run_gh` — that part of the contract is unchanged and is what makes this a promotion rather than a rewrite.

**The revision-1 acceptance criterion "all fifteen existing tests pass unmodified" is WITHDRAWN and STAYS withdrawn.** It was the wrong criterion: it made behaviour-preservation the goal, and behaviour-preservation is exactly what produced the unsafe default AM-4 removed. **A test that must change is the honest signal that behaviour changed**, and suppressing that signal is worse than the edit it saves. **What changes in revision 5 is not the criterion but the size of the set it applies to, and the direction of the change is down — from six tests to one.**

> **REVISION 5: the "six existing tests must change" obligation COLLAPSES TO ONE, and this is a finding rather than a saving. It is stated here in full because a reviewer who remembers AM-28(d)'s six-test scope will otherwise read the reduction as work being dropped.**
>
> Revision 4's six rows existed for **one** reason between them: **the milestone-object skip rule** (AM-18). Four `TestProbeRepoMilestone` fixtures and the vacuous sixth were broken by the skip — records built by `_make_issue()` (**`:42-46`**) carry no `milestone` object, so the skip dropped them *before* `_verify_codeowner_actor` was reached. **AM-35 retires the skip.** With the skip gone the records reach the patched function again, `mock_verify_codeowner.assert_called_once()` passes, and **all five of those rows need no edit at all.**
>
> **The sixth row — the vacuous one — un-vacuums itself, and that is the most important consequence.** `TestProbeRepoMilestone::test_skips_issue_with_no_verified_codeowner_actor` (`:318-335`) is **#1539's own regression test**. Under revision 4 it passed **for the wrong reason**: the skip dropped the record before the authorization check ran, so `results == []` proved nothing about the subject in its docstring. **With the skip retired, the record reaches `_verify_codeowner_actor`, the patched `None` is consulted, and the test exercises exactly what it is named for.** AM-28(d)'s remedy (the shared `_make_issue()` fix) is therefore **not needed** — the defect it was written to repair is removed by a different ruling, in the same change set. **A coder must not apply AM-28(d)'s fixture fix "for completeness": it would be repairing a fixture for a rule that no longer exists.**

**The one test that must change, and it changes because a channel was deleted rather than because a default was inverted:**

| Test | Why it changes | Required edit |
|---|---|---|
| `TestCodeownersActorVerification::test_verified_when_milestone_applied_by_codeowner_human` (`:476-483`) | **It asserts that a `milestoned` event authorizes. Under AR-7 / AM-35 the `milestoned` arm is DELETED**, so the function returns `None` for that input. AM-37's RP-8 disposition names this test specifically: it *"tests a channel that is gone."* | **INVERT it, do not delete it.** Rename to `test_milestoned_event_never_authorizes` and assert that a `milestoned` event by a verified individual human CODEOWNER, for any milestone, returns `None`. **Deleting it would remove the only mechanical statement that the channel is gone**; inverting it converts the test that proved the channel worked into the test that proves it was removed. The `_milestoned_event()` helper (**`:465`**) is retained as the fixture that feeds it and **is NOT extended to emit a `milestone` object** — revision 4 required that extension to exercise AM-4's title binding, and there is no longer a title to bind. |

**Three things follow that a reviewer should hold me to.**

1. **Revision 1's acceptance criterion was already false for the four `TestProbeRepoMilestone` fixtures, and that remains my defect even though the rulings have now made the symptom disappear.** I wrote a skip rule into revision 1's §1.6 and did not check the file's fixtures against it. **The fixtures are fine again by accident of a later ruling, not by my having caught it** — and a defect that stops manifesting is not a defect that was found. It stays recorded in §7.3.
2. **`test_probe_passes_expected_milestone_title` is RETIRED from the added-test list below**, along with `test_probe_milestoned_event_for_a_different_title_does_not_authorize`: both assert the behaviour of a parameter this revision deletes. Their retirement is AM-35's, not a scope reduction of mine.
3. **The panel's `{"title"}` measurement survives its tests.** 97/97 `milestoned` payloads carrying exactly `{"title"}` (panel RE-RUN §2) is preserved in §0.6 gap 5's retirement note and travels to S3's `demilestoned` rule (AM-37, RP-8: *"a measurement thrown away is a measurement re-taken"*). **What is retired is the fixture work, not the observation.**

The replacement acceptance criterion for "one implementation" is narrower and actually checkable, and it is **unchanged** by the collapse: **every existing assertion either passes unmodified or is changed only in its fixture, and every changed test is listed in the PR body with the sentence "this test changed because the behaviour changed."** Any test whose *assertion* must weaken is a defect in the change, not in the test — **with one named exception, which is the row above: `test_verified_when_milestone_applied_by_codeowner_human`'s assertion is INVERTED, not weakened, and the PR body must say so in those words.** The criterion also still applies to any test asserting the exact `_run_gh` argument list, which changes when `--paginate` is added.

**No other `probe.py` behaviour changes in S1.** In particular, the milestone strategy does **not** gain `requester_verdict`: it already requires a verified CODEOWNER actor for *every* issue, which is strictly stricter than the gate's trusted-author fast path. Adding requester-trust there would only *widen* it. Recorded as OBS-1, §7.1.

## 2. S2 — the deterministic selection gate

### 2.1 File layout

| Path | Status | Purpose |

| `scripts/automation/lib/next_candidates.jq` | **deleted** | Absorbed. |
| `bin/hos-cron` | **modified** (`:1131-1146`, plus comments at `:1132`, `:1587`) | Calls the entry point. |
| `bootstrap/worker-cron-prompt.md` | **modified** (Step 2 fallback, `:97-101`) | Calls the same entry point. |
| `.claude/agents/worker.md` | **modified** (`:179`, `:271`) | Stops naming `next_candidates.jq` as the ordering implementation. |
| `scripts/framework/framework_consumer_files.txt` | **modified** — **NEW in revision 4 (AM-26, panel P-4)** | **Two lines added: `scripts/framework/select_work_candidates.py` and `scripts/framework/requester_trust.py`**, in the same change set that creates them — not a follow-up. **`scripts/framework/trusted-requesters.txt` is deliberately NOT added**, and that is a decision, not an omission: see the ruling below the table. |
| `docs/LABELS.md` | **modified** (`:11`, `:29`, `:30`, `:49`, plus the `needs-ai` row) | Hardcoded-literal inventory updated; **AM-6's required statement**, now in its AM-25 form (`/approve` changes **no** label and authorizes nothing); **AM-15's authorization-record sentence**; and **NEW in revision 4 — AM-27 point 2: the milestone-route prohibition lands here**, with the Step 0 race as its stated reason. This file is the **durable reference home** for the authorizing act. **No new label** (AD-11). |

Control 1 today tells the human to intervene by *"removing `needs-human`, adding `needs-ai`"* **with no statement that the act must be performed from their own account** — re-read by me this session (§0.0.3). Post-S2 that instruction is a documented approval path that can silently do nothing, which AM-1 names as worse than no gate. §4.3 carries the three facts the fix must add. |
| `.github/workflows/label-swap.yml` | **modified** (`:74` — the write is **removed**; `:75` — the body is replaced; **`:79`'s `/decline` sibling left alone**) | **Changed in revision 4 — AM-25 supersedes revision 3's disposition.** Revision 3 left the label swap live and fixed only the confirmation wording, on three stated reasons. **Two of the three are false** (§2.7.2), so the basis is gone and the disposition goes with it. **In S2, `/approve`'s branch performs NO label write.** The `2>/dev/null \|\| true` at `:74` goes with the write it guards. `/decline` is untouched. See §2.7.2. |
| `tests/automation/test_hos_cron.py` | **modified** — **NEW in revision 4 (AM-28(a))** | Not a protected surface, and named here because the panel found it **missing from every list in revision 3 while three binding test rows depend on it**. `bin/hos-cron`'s behaviour cannot be tested from `test_select_work_candidates.py`; **F25, F30 and AM-12's `test_bounced_draft_pr_still_reaches_the_worker_and_blocks_the_skip` live here** (§6). |

> **REVISION 5: the five surfaces are CLEARED by the human (H1 item 4, #1540 2026-09-16T07:14:38Z), and the clearance is scoped exactly as this section enumerates them.** *"Accepted as scoped. The sixth surface (`CLAUDE.md`, human-proxy self-label behavior) stays **separate** — land it as its own follow-up change, not folded into this PR. Preference for smaller, discrete work items over bundling."* **Two consequences, and the second is the one that could go wrong quietly.** (i) The count is **five** and is now cleared at five rather than merely argued at five — §2.7.1's exclusion of option D is ratified by the person it costs, which is the only ratification that counts for a scope boundary. (ii) **A later editor who folds the `CLAUDE.md` change into S2 "since it is small" is reversing a human ruling, not tidying a scope line**, and the count becomes six with no clearance behind it. **Clearance is not pre-authorization of any individual merge** — AD-14 stands, each of the five is still human-gated at merge, and nothing in this document authorizes any of them.
>
> **The `trusted-requesters.txt.example` file is GIVEN ITS HOME in §1.1 (RP-19) and is created in S1.** It sits inside `scripts/framework/**`, so it adds no surface and does not disturb the cleared count. Revision 4 ruled it into the ship list (§4.3) and listed it in no layout table; that defect is closed in §1.1, not here, and it is named here only so a reviewer checking the ship list against the layout tables finds them agreeing.

`bin/hos-cron`, `bootstrap/**`, `scripts/framework/**`, `.claude/agents/**` **and `.github/workflows/**`** are all protected surfaces. **Five** human-gated merges. **The count is unchanged by revision 4, Amendment 3 confirms it explicitly, Amendment 4 confirms it again (AM-34's "What this is NOT"), and the human has now cleared it**: `framework_consumer_files.txt` is a *new file inside an existing surface* (`scripts/framework/**`), and `label-swap.yml` was already being edited — AM-25 changes *what* that edit is, not how many surfaces S2 touches. `docs/LABELS.md`, `docs/OVERSIGHT-RUNBOOK.md`, `tests/automation/test_hos_cron.py` and `SCRIPTS-INDEX.md` are edited and are **not** protected surfaces. None of the five is pre-authorized by this document (AD-14).

> **The consumer ship set — binding, and the second half is the part that matters (AM-26, panel P-4).**
>
> **Verified by me this session** (§0.0.3): `framework_consumer_files.txt` ships `bin/hos-cron` at `:20` and enumerates `scripts/framework/` modules one by one at `:67-69` — including `require_human_approval.py`, the very module `requester_trust.py` will import. **It contains no entry for any of S2's three new files.** The failure that produces is invisible from this repo and worth stating in full, because it is the shape of defect a design review exists to catch: a consumer upgrading to a release carrying S2 receives the new `bin/hos-cron`, whose candidates block runs `python3 -m scripts.framework.select_work_candidates` against a tree that does not contain it. `ModuleNotFoundError` → `_gate_candidates_ok=0` → **no new work, every cycle**, with a bare Python traceback carrying **no `$LOG_PREFIX` and no `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` token** — so the one operator-legible line S2 adds never fires, and DEV-2's whole purpose is defeated for exactly the deployment that cannot diagnose it.
>
> **1. The two modules are added.** They are HOS-owned code and an upgrade *should* overwrite them.
>
> **2. The roster is NOT added, and the reason is stronger than "the design does not say."** `bootstrap/hos_install.sh`'s `cp_framework_file()` is an **unconditional `cp`** with no destination guard — read by me this session; its own comment says skipping files *"defeats the purpose of running an upgrade."* Every listed file is therefore clobbered on every upgrade, silently. Listing a **consumer-maintained trust roster** there would mean an HOS upgrade **silently deletes every trusted contributor a consumer added**. That fails closed on trust — and it is a *silent, unrequested change to a trust boundary*, which is the one category of change this entire ADR exists to prevent. **The house line is already drawn exactly here and I am following it, not inventing it:** `scripts/framework/machine-accounts.env` (consumer-specific config) is deliberately absent from that list while `protected_surfaces.txt` (HOS-owned policy) is present at `:57` — both verified by me. F6 already makes an absent roster benign: empty set, gate runs, CODEOWNERS and apps still confer trust (FR3).
>
> **The reason must travel with the list entry, not only with this document.** Amendment 3's own risk register names *"the roster being added to `framework_consumer_files.txt` by a later contributor for completeness"* as a way this ruling gets silently undone. **Binding: the two added lines carry a comment in the file stating why the roster is absent** — one sentence, in the same register as the file's existing section comments. A decision recorded only in a design document is a decision the next contributor will not find.
>
> **3. A `.example` template, if one is shipped at all, MUST be at a different path.** Never the live one. I rule that **one is shipped**: `scripts/framework/trusted-requesters.txt.example`, added to the ship list, carrying §1.3's format and the same header the live file carries. The alternative — documenting the format only in prose — makes a consumer hand-construct a file whose parser rejects malformed lines silently-but-for-a-diagnostic, and the format is exactly the kind of thing `.example` files exist for. The consumer-facing documentation of the roster is required either way (§4.3).
>
> **4. S2 creates a new consumer precondition, and nothing states it.** Step B1/B3 exit 2 on an absent or empty `machine-accounts.env`, and that file is **not shipped and not generated by the installer** — verified by me: it is absent from `framework_consumer_files.txt`, and `provision_agent_account.sh` *dies* if it is missing. **Today a consumer can run cron cycles without it** (it is needed only by the merge gates). **After S2, its absence stops work selection entirely.** That is a real change to the consumer contract. It fails closed and it is visible via DEV-2 — but it must be **written down** rather than discovered by a consumer whose worker goes quiet. §4.3 places it in two homes: the **S2 work item (#1539)** and the **consumer-facing upgrade note in the S2 PR body**.
>
> **And the detector, because P-4's real lesson is that nothing would have caught this.** The existing guard test checks only that *listed* files exist, so it cannot see a listed file whose **dependency** is unlisted. **§6.3 gains a test asserting that every `scripts/framework/**` module imported by a shipped module is itself shipped** — for this change set at minimum, `select_work_candidates.py` → `requester_trust.py` → `require_human_approval.py`. Without it, P-4 recurs the next time a shipped module grows an import, and it recurs invisibly from this repo.

> **Ruled: the count is FIVE (AM-17 — §8/E-6 UPHELD in full).** Revision 2's enumeration above was already correct, so **revision 3 ratifies it rather than changing it**, and deliberately does not re-derive it: the surface list is checked against `scripts/framework/protected_surfaces.txt`, and that check was made by revision 2 and re-made by the architect. Three things the ruling settles, each of which was an open question in revision 2: (i) **S2 does touch `.github/workflows/**`** — AM-6 point 3 is a shipping condition and the confirmation comment the workflow posts is the artefact that violates it, so the edit is in scope; (ii) **S2 is not blocked** by the extra surface — the architect declined the "then S2 does not ship" branch explicitly, because blocking a `priority:critical` fix over a one-surface enumeration would be worse than either the fix or the enumeration; (iii) **there is no sixth** — `CLAUDE.md` (option D) stays out of S1/S2 for the reason §2.7.1 gives, which the architect confirmed, and is named to the human as a **planned follow-up** so a sixth surface arriving later is expected rather than a surprise. The correction to what the human is asked to clear is made in **Amendment 2 §3(b) item 4**, which is the text that must reach them; Amendment 1's four-surface text is superseded and must not be quoted.

### 2.2 CLI contract

```
python3 -m scripts.framework.select_work_candidates \
    --repo <owner/repo> \
    --milestone <positive integer> \
    [--label <name>]                        # default: needs-ai
    [--max-candidates <n>]                  # EARLY-EXIT stop. Default 5 in revision 5
                                            #   (was 10); may only lower.
    [--max-api-requests <n>]                # RENAMED AND RE-DENOMINATED in revision 5
                                            #   (AM-30). A per-cycle GitHub API REQUEST
                                            #   budget, not a count of checks.
                                            #   Default 100; may only lower.
```

**Two flag changes in revision 5, and neither is a new flag — the count stays at exactly five.**

- **`--max-authorization-checks` → `--max-api-requests` (AM-30, binding).** The old name denominated the bound in *checks*, and AM-5 makes one check **1 to 10 requests**, so a ceiling of 100 checks was a ceiling of 100–1,000 requests — *"a bound whose unit is not the rationed resource cannot bound the rationed resource."* **The number does not move and this is not a raise:** ≈100, derived from the 5,000/hour installation limit and the twelve-cycle/hour cadence, `min(100, max(0, provided))`. **The rename is load-bearing rather than cosmetic** — a flag called `--max-authorization-checks` invites a reader to reason in checks, which is the exact reasoning that produced E-8.
- **`--max-candidates` default 10 → 5**, and the number is **not chosen**: it is `bin/hos-cron`'s own appetite, read at **`:1892-1895`** this revision (*"head -5 caps display"*, then `printf '%s\n' "$_GATE_CANDIDATES" | head -5`). Revision 4 set 10 as *"deliberate headroom so that the stop is never the thing that decides which issue is built"*; under the human's early-exit directive the stop **is** now the thing that ends the walk, so a default that is twice what the consumer reads is twice the paid work for candidates nobody looks at. **The clamp is unchanged: `min(5, max(1, provided))`, and the lower clamp is 1, not 0.**

**stdout** — zero or more lines, byte-identical in format to the deleted jq filter:

```
#<number> [<critical|high|medium|low>] <title>
```

**stderr** — diagnostics and exactly one summary line. Never candidate data.

**Exit codes — THREE in revision 5, and the third is AM-32's requirement made mechanical:**

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `0` | A determination the gate can **state the extent of**, with at least one candidate emitted **or** with `complete=yes`. **The SET of candidates on stdout is exactly the first `k` records the ranked WALK over the fetched set DETERMINED eligible, in walk order** (*"determined" added in revision 7, RP4-1: a record the walk stepped over without a determination is named on its own `unevaluated:query-failed issue=#<n>` line and forces `complete=no`); **stdout PRESENTS that set in the EMISSION order `(rank, number ASC)`, which is a different order and is therefore NOT itself a prefix of the walk** (§2.3 D5.1, restated in revision 6 ~~per the human's TP-4 ruling~~ *as a correction under the human's TP-4 classification, on the panel's recommended limb; attribution corrected in revision 7, RP4-8*). `complete=<yes\|no>` says whether the fetched set was the whole of the answer. |
| **`3`** | **DEGRADED — NEW (AM-32). `complete=no` AND stdout EMPTY.** The gate ran, reached no usable candidate, and **cannot say there is none.** The caller MUST NOT read this as "no actionable work". It is not an error and it is not fail-closed: the security determination is intact and every record the gate did evaluate was evaluated correctly. |
| `2` | **Fail closed.** stdout is empty. stderr carries one `select_work_candidates: <reason-token>` line. The caller must treat this as "no candidates", never as "unknown, proceed anyway". |

`argparse`'s own usage-error exit is also `2`, which is consistent: a malformed invocation is a fail-closed condition.

**Exit 3's condition is exact, and the precision is what keeps it from destroying work (AM-32, binding):**

> **Exit 3 if and only if `complete=no` AND the emitted candidate list is EMPTY. In every other case the gate exits 0.**
>
> **This rule governs the all-gated case too, and nothing overrides it (revision 7, RUN4 RP4-2).** When every evaluated candidate is gated (`eligible == 0`, `gated > 0`), the exit is **0 if `complete=yes`** and **3 if `complete=no`**. The `ALL-CANDIDATES-GATED` block fires in both cases, carrying its fourth `INCOMPLETE` line when `complete=no` (§2.3 Step F, F30). Through revision 6, F30 said *"exit 0 … Never an exit code"* while explicitly covering `complete=no`, and its test pinned exit 0. That contradicted this row on the path the cutover takes most often. At 149 candidates against a ≈100-request ceiling (RUN4 M-1), **a fully-gated walk cannot reach `complete=yes`**, so F30's exit 0 would have handed `bin/hos-cron` an empty stdout with exit 0. That is the *"no actionable work"* reading AM-32 exists to prevent. **The held condition does not choose the exit; completeness does.**

**Why it is conditioned on an empty stdout rather than on `complete=no` alone — this is a design decision of mine and the reason is mechanical, not stylistic.** ~~`bin/hos-cron`'s call site captures with `if _GATE_CANDIDATES=$( … )`~~ `bin/hos-cron`'s call site branches on the gate's status with an explicit `case "$_gate_rc"` (§2.4), and **every non-zero arm clears `_GATE_CANDIDATES` and sets `_gate_candidates_ok=0`**. *(Caller description corrected in revision 7, RP4-6(g). The `if _GATE_CANDIDATES=$(…)` form is today's call site, which §2.4 replaces. The reasoning is unchanged, because the replacement preserves "any non-zero status discards the candidates".)* A gate that exited 3 while holding usable candidates would **throw those candidates away** — turning an incompleteness *report* into a work-selection *failure*, and doing it on the single most common path in the design, because the routine early-exit stop always sets `complete=no`. **The state AM-32 names is precisely "incomplete and empty":** *"AM-31 would, unamended, convert 'the gate could not determine anything' into 'there is nothing to do'."* When the list is non-empty the caller cannot make that mistake — it has work — and `complete=no` on stderr is the right register for a fact the operator needs and the control flow does not.

**A reconciliation this document owes its own earlier sections, stated rather than left for a reader to trip over.** §1.3.1's row for `fetch_collaborators` and §1.3.2's failure rows say a tier-resolution failure yields *"exit 3 (degraded)"*. **Read exactly, that is shorthand:** a tier-resolution failure **contributes the degraded condition** — it sets `complete=no` and emits `WARN tier-resolution-failed` — and that condition **becomes exit 3 exactly when the gate has nothing to emit.** If the degraded run still finds a usable candidate, the exit is 0, the `WARN` and `complete=no` still fire, and the caller proceeds with real work rather than discarding it. **The two sections are consistent under this definition and inconsistent under any reading in which "degraded" is itself an exit code**; §2.2 is the definitive statement and §1.3.1/§1.3.2 are its consumers.

**The criterion that decides which exit a failure or bound produces — AM-31, which REPLACES AM-21 (binding, new in revision 5).** AM-21 asked what the gate *knows* about an omission (is it enumerable? is it ordered?) and never asked what the omission *costs*. Two bounds that fail the same test can carry opposite harms, so a criterion blind to the harm produces a rule that is too strict in one place and too loose in another — which is the state RP-5 and RP-7 described from opposite sides.

> **THE CRITERION (AM-31, verbatim in substance).** **A failure or bound that can affect the *security* determination — whether any record is treated as authorized — MUST exit 2.** **A failure or bound that affects only the *completeness* or the *ordering* of the emitted set MUST NOT stop the world: it reports the omission — what class, how much, or that the extent is unknown — and the walk continues.** *"Fail-closed means never authorize on an unknown; it does not mean stop the world on an unknown, and the difference is the whole of this ruling."*

**What this changes, concretely, and it is one row of §3 rather than a family:** **D5.3's events-query failure no longer exits 2.** The record is **quarantined** under `unevaluated:query-failed`, named with its issue number, **never counted as `gated`**, and the ranked walk continues. `probe.py:298-303` already ships that behaviour (`try/except GitHubError: return None`); **the gate's exit 2 was the novel behaviour, not the quarantine.** Step B's configuration exits are **unchanged at exit 2** — a wrong trust, bot or CODEOWNER set changes every record's determination and can do so permissively — and F1 … F12 stand exactly as written. §3's matrix is read against this criterion, and **AM-21's two-limb wording is retired from this document**; where an older cross-reference cites it, §3's F13 row carries the replacement.

**One further binding limb of AM-31, easy to lose because it is a sentence about arithmetic:** **the gate MUST NOT state a truncation count it cannot derive.** GitHub's `/issues` returns no total, so a Step C truncation reports *"at least 500 records matched; the total is unknown"* — **never a number the gate computed by assuming the next page is full.** A `Link: rel="last"` probe could bound it; that is an optional improvement, explicitly **not** binding, and it must not become a reason to delay.

#### 2.2.1 The gate's three bounds (AM-22, binding), the early exit (the human's directive), and the request counter (AM-30)

> **REVISION 5 RESTRUCTURES THIS SECTION AROUND FOUR THINGS THAT ARRIVED AFTER REVISION 4, and the order below is the order a reader needs them in.** (1) **The walk early-exits** — the human's 15:46:50Z directive 1, which narrows AM-19's walk-to-the-ceiling shape without touching AM-29's order, AM-30's unit or AM-31's criterion. (2) **The cost bound is denominated in API requests** (AM-30), which requires a counter this document must define rather than assume — RP-21's off-by-one is subsumed by that definition. (3) **The walk order and the emission order are different orders** (AM-29). (4) **Tier resolution adds 0–3 requests per cycle** to the same budget (H3, §1.3.2). **The measured population underneath all four is 101, not 97 or 98** (§0.0.4).
>
> **REVISION 6 CHANGES EXACTLY ONE OF THOSE FOUR, AND ONLY ITS KEY.** Item (3) stands — **the two orders are still different decisions and Step E is still load-bearing** — but the walk key is now **`(rank, number DESC)`**, adopted from AM-29's own stated fallback on the human's TP-1 ruling. **Items (1), (2) and (4) are untouched.** The two orders are now exact reverses within a band, so they disagree on *every* same-rank pair rather than only on pairs whose recency and numbering disagree — **which makes §6.2's "the two orders disagree" fixture trivial to construct and makes a re-fusion of the two keys easier to catch, not harder.**

##### The early exit — what the human asked for, what it buys, and the one thing it must not be read as

**The directive, quoted rather than paraphrased** (#1540, 2026-09-16T15:46:50Z, relayed through the human-proxy App):

> *"Walk candidates in the already-ruled order (AM-29's `(rank, updated_at DESC, number ASC)`) and **stop at the first usable one** — don't keep evaluating the rest this cycle. The next cycle continues from where this one left off."*

> **REVISION 6 — the key named inside that quotation is SUPERSEDED, and the quotation is not edited (AM-38).** The human wrote it on 2026-09-16 at 15:46:50Z, when AM-29's primary binding was the walk key; they replaced that key themselves on 2026-09-16 at 19:55:45Z after the panel's TP-1. **The directive's own content — *stop at the first usable one* — is entirely unaffected: it is a statement about WHEN the walk stops, not about the order it walks in.** The binding walk key everywhere in this document is **`(rank, number DESC)`**.

**Binding: the walk stops as soon as the eligible set reaches `--max-candidates` (default 5), and the stop ends the walk COMPLETELY — no further record is admitted, trusted or otherwise.** This is mechanically the same stop revision 4 already had; **what changes is its default and its status.** In revision 4 the sufficiency stop was headroom deliberately set high so it *"is never the thing that decides which issue is built"*; in revision 5 it is the **primary cost control**, and the request ceiling becomes the worst-case backstop behind it.

**Why the stop is at `--max-candidates` and NOT literally at one, which is what the directive says.** `bootstrap/worker-cron-prompt.md:96` — read this revision — instructs the worker to *"**Pick the first non-blocked candidate** (#901)."* **The worker's documented contract is to skip a blocked candidate, and that requires more than one candidate to be offered.** A literal stop-at-one converts every blocked head-of-queue issue into a **head-of-line stall**: the gate emits one candidate, the worker declines it as blocked, and the cycle does nothing — every cycle, until a human intervenes on that one issue. **Five is the consumer's own appetite** (`bin/hos-cron:1892-1895`'s `head -5`), so stopping at five is *"stop at the first usable one"* expressed in the unit the caller actually consumes. **This is a design decision of mine on top of the human's directive, so it is stated rather than absorbed**, and §9 hands it to the panel.

**What the early exit actually buys at the present population, measured rather than asserted (§0.0.4, worker, 2026-09-16T~15:55Z).** Of the human's three efficiency directives, **only this one reduces cost today** — and it reduces it a great deal:

| Directive | Measured effect at a population of 101 | Disposition |
|---|---|---|
| **Early exit** | **Up to 101 paid verifications per cycle → as few as 1.** The saving is the whole of the human's expected *"well below the ~105-request worst case"* | **Implemented. It is sufficient on its own** |
| **`needs-human` exclusion** | **Removes ZERO records.** `next_candidates.jq:22` has excluded them since `#901`, and 0 of 101 carry the label | **Implemented as D2 and already present. A free correctness guard, NOT a cost saving, and it must not be credited with any of the efficiency the human expects** |
| **Trusted-author fast path** | **Skips 4 of 101** under the safe reading — `ScottThurlow`'s own filings, trusted by **AD-1(a)**, which is existing ruled design and not new. The roster ships empty and adds none | **Implemented as the existing D3/D4. See §8/E-10: the literal reading would skip 97 of 101 and make the gate inert on this repo's entire present queue** |

**The check the human asked for explicitly, performed and recorded: the early exit does NOT conflict with AM-29's rank dominance or with R-8's mitigations.** The directive says *"`technical-design` should verify this doesn't conflict with AM-29's rank-dominance/R-8 mitigations — reading it, it shouldn't."* **It does not, and the argument is two lines rather than a judgement:**

1. **Rank dominance is a property of the walk's *order*, not of its *length*.** The walk visits every rank-0 record before any rank-1 record, for any total order of the form `(rank, X)` (AM-29's own reasoning). **Stopping early truncates the tail of that order**, so everything the stop left unreached is at or below the rank of everything admitted. ~~*Nothing omitted can outrank anything emitted* survives a stop at any position, including position 1.~~ **CORRECTED IN REVISION 7 (RUN4 RP4-1):** *nothing the stop left UNREACHED can outrank anything admitted*, and that survives a stop at any position, including position 1. **The unqualified "nothing omitted" was false.** A record the walk *reached* but could not determine (D5.3's quarantine, AM-31) is omitted, can outrank an admitted record, and is named on its own `unevaluated:query-failed issue=#<n>` line. **The stop did not cause that; the hole did.** §2.3 D5.1 states rank dominance over determinations. The early-exit conclusion is unchanged: the stop can only remove records ranked at or below what it admitted.
2. **R-8's mitigations are untouched by the early exit — but there are now TWO of them, not three, and the withdrawal of the third is a revision-6 correction rather than a re-derivation.** (i) The record must already be in the target milestone **and** carry the dispatch label to be a candidate at all — unchanged by any stop. (ii) **Rank dominates position-in-band absolutely**, so a `priority:critical` record is never displaced by anything happening in a lower band — and an early exit cannot change that, because it can only remove *lower*-ranked records from consideration.

   > **MITIGATION (iii) IS WITHDRAWN — stated plainly rather than deleted, because a mitigation that disappears without a sentence is the object this chain keeps finding.** Revision 5 wrote: *"The human's authorizing act is itself an update, so it lands at the head of its band — and with an early exit that head position is now worth more, not less, because the walk reaches fewer records and reaching the head is what matters."* **That sentence is now false, and it is false for the reason it was always fragile: the key it rested on was publicly writable.**
   >
   > **Why it is withdrawn.** The panel's **TP-1** — raised independently by all four chunks of the adversarial lens and verified live against #1709 — established that **`issue.updated_at` advances when anyone comments**, which on a public repository is any GitHub account with no permission beyond commenting. So the authorizing act's promotion was never exclusive: **one comment on each gated record in the same band pushes the authorized record back down, indefinitely, for free.** The human ruled: *"Switch to AM-29's own stated fallback `(rank, number DESC)` — immune to the comment-bump attack. **Accept losing the promotion-on-authorization property as the cost of closing a zero-privilege, indefinitely-repeatable starvation vector in a security-fix mechanism. Security posture over convenience here.**"*
   >
   > **What R-8's residual mitigation actually is now — the honest version, since the ruling removes the recovery act that made R-8 comfortable to accept.** Mitigations (i) and (ii) stand as written and are **both structural rather than incidental**: (i) is a triage-privilege precondition the attacker must already have defeated, and (ii) is a property of any `(rank, X)` order. **The replacement for (iii) is that the walk key is no longer influenceable at all** — `number` is assigned by GitHub at creation and never changes, so **there is no act, by anyone, that moves a record within its band.** That is a *stronger* property than (iii) claimed against an attacker and a *weaker* one for the human: **the attack is closed and the recovery act is gone.**
   >
   > **REVISION 7 (RUN4 RP4-5, documentary part): the paragraph below OVERCLAIMS and is corrected rather than deleted.** "The one recovery act that survives" is not true as a general statement. **Within a band there is no recovery act at all. For rank 0 (`priority:critical`) there is none, because there is no higher rank to move to.** An old critical behind `--max-candidates` or more newer eligible criticals is never reached by any act. **For ranks 1–3 a re-rank is at best a partial act.** The record enters its new band at its own (low) number, so under `number DESC` it lands at that band's **tail**. It is reached only if fewer than `--max-candidates` eligible records precede it in the walk and the request budget lasts that far. In practice it works only when the destination band is nearly empty. **The human's question on whether this cost was part of what the TP-1 ruling accepted was put on #1540 at 2026-09-25T07:16:34Z and is PENDING.** This revision does not answer it (§7.2 R-8).
   >
   > **The one recovery act that survives, and it is mine to state rather than the ruling's** *(superseded as a general claim by the note above)*. Because rank is the first key and dominates absolutely, **a record can still be moved forward — by changing its rank**, i.e. applying a higher `priority:*` label. **That is a triage-privileged write, which the untrusted author does not have** (it is the same privilege class as mitigation (i)'s precondition), so it is a recovery act and not a second gaming vector. **It is coarser than (iii) was** — it moves a record a whole band rather than to the head of its own — **and it changes a record's stated priority, which is a real cost: an operator using it to un-starve a record is mislabelling that record's priority to work around a walk order.** §7.2 R-8 carries this as a residual rather than as a mitigation, because a workaround that requires misstating data is not a mitigation.

**What the early exit does NOT do, stated because it is the obvious misreading of "the next cycle continues from where this one left off".** **The gate keeps NO cursor, NO resume token and NO state of any kind** (FR7/AD-4).
